=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosureno
Embassy, Moscow, U.S.S.R., on the
subject of the new government in
Japan.
SoURCE: Izvestiya, Moscow, January 9, 1939.
TOrgan of the Soviets of Deputies
of the Toilers of the U.S.S.R.)
TRANSLATION.
NEW GOVERNNENT IN JAPAN.
Japan's governmental crisis was ended by the forma-
tion of Baron Kiitsiro Hiranuma's Cabinet. The charao-
OF
ter of the new cabinet becomes clear if the basic reasons
for the fall of Prince Fumimaro Konoye's Cabinet are con-
sidered.
One of the more serious reasons for the governmental
crisis was the failure of Konoye's policy in China. It
should be recalled that Konoye's Government was formed in
June 1937, i.e., inu ediately prior to the beginning of
military operations in China. The prolonged war in China
has led to the international isolation of Japan and
brought its economy, especially its finances, to a sit-
uation vergin on bankruptcy. Within the country dis-
satisfaction with the military adventure is increasing.
Even among the ruling classes serious differences were
noted. These differences were the iimediate reason for
the dovmfall of Konoye's Cabinet.
Along what lines did these conflicts proceed?
In the first place, differences were occasioned
over the question of the further mobilization of means
for conducting the war. It should not be forgotten that
differences arose precisely on the eve of the inauguration
of the 74th Session of the Diet, which was to have passed
on the Government's budget. The plan for the 1939/1940
budget

=== Page 18 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
budget provides for expenditures in the amount of 3,690
million yen. In ad ition to t is the Army and Navy
Ministries presented a project for expenditures in the
amount of 6 billion yen for the conduct of the war in
China.
The ouestion arose: By what revenues can these
enormous expenditures be met? Taxes alone can no longer
satisfy all the increasin: demands of the military. Con-
su ption of goods has fallen catastrophically in Japan
and a number of branches of industry have sharply cut
production due to a lack of raw materials. From these,
indirect and in part direct taxes are in arrears.
The floatin of enormous state loans, on account
of the disinclination of the powerful financial magnates
to risk their profits, is becoming more and more diffi-
cult each day. An ever-increaring quantity of bonds
remains in the portfolios of the Bank of Japan. Mean-
while, in 1938-39 loans in the amount of 5,628 million
yen should have been issued, and for 1939-40 the amount
should be 6,8l0 million yen.
On December 13, 1938, the Minister for Finance
decided to put into operation article ll of the law for
the "General Mobilization of the Country", which provides
for the limiting of dividends from all companies having
a capital of 200,000 yen or more to 10%, in order that
the Government may dispose of the balance of the profits
at its own will. The companies, through their repre-
sentatives in the Seiyukai and Minseito Parties, began
to threaten the Government and the military with opposi-
tion speeches at the 74th Session of the Diet. The
military,

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
military, in their turn, demanded from Konoye "extra-
ordinary" measures in relation to the Diet, even before
the opening of the latter's session.
These "extraordinary" measures were formulated by
the ardent Fascist Minister for Internal Affairs, Ad-
miral Suetsugu. According to the Tokyo correspondent
of the Reuter Agency, he demanded the dismissal of the
Diet and the dissolution of the bourgeois Seiyukai and
Minseito Parties, as well as the formation of a single
Fascist Party, in which the Government should find its
support. At the same time, dissatisfaction increased in
those business and i dustrial circles which are suffer-
ing heavy losses as a result of the state control based
on the law for the "General Mobilization of the Country".
Caught between the pincers of the demands of the mili-
tary on the one side and the opposition of the important
financial circles on the other, Konoye tendered his resig-
nation.
Under these circuastances the selection of Hira-
numa was not accidental. Hiranuma is a well-known re-
actionary, the organizer and head of the Fascist organiza-
tion "Kokuhonsha", in which are enrolled such notorious
Fascists as Generals Araki, Madzaki, Admiral Suetsugu
and others. In addition to these connections, Hiranuma
has old ties with the court bureaucracy, and through
these he is linked also with the financial circles.
Hiranumm apparently is considered to be the sort of
a person who is desired by the military as well as by
the court bureaucracy and financial circles. However,
his appointment as premier signifies, above all, a new
victory

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
victory for the aggressive military. It is evident
that the court bureaucracy and big capitalists are making
a move in the direction of still closer relations with
the Fascist military. It is characteristic that when
the former chairman of the Privy Council, Kuratomi, re-
signed in 1934, he was replaced by Ikki, a representa-
tive of the liberal win of the court bureaucracy and
not by Hiranuma, who at that time had already served
eight years as vice chairman of the Privy Council.
Then, all at once, Hiranuma was placed in the of-
fice of chairnan of the Secret Council by the military
Fascist putsch of February 26, 1936, which was directed
especially against the old court bureaucracy, i.e., Ikki,
I.akino, Saito and others. Nevertheless, even at that
tie he (and it follows alco the military) was conpelled
to make a "concession" to the old bureaucracy and to
discolve formally the "Kokuhonsha". Prior to the war
in China, his candi? ture for the post of nremier was
proposed several ti es by the military and each time it
vas rejected by the Genro Saionji, "patriarch" of the
court bureaucr cy. Now, that same Saionji has recommen-
ded Hiranuma for the post of premier. Thus the fascisized
military are drawing nearer and nearer to their aim: to
become the single political representative of the bloc
of finance, capital, landowners and court bureaucracy,
with no bourgeois parties and no parliament.
But even this time, it was necessary to make con-
cessions: Admiral Suetsugu resigned and his post as
Minister for Internal Affairs was taken by Marquis Kido,
formerly Minister for Public Health and Education, and
closely

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
closely connected ith the court circles, for up to
1937 he was chief secretary to the Lord Keeper of the
Seal.
A concession to the financial circles was the ap-
pointment to the Cabinet of Sakurautsi and Maeda, im-
portant representatives of the Minseito and Seiukai
Parties.
Among other new appointnents there should be noted
the designation as Minister for Finance of Isivatari
Sotaro, an old functionary of the Finance Ministry. It
is characteristic that on every occasion when the fi-
nancial demands of the military encounter opposition from
the powerful concerns, a functionary, who has few con-
nections with financial circles is put in as Finance
Minister and
not a financier of the type of Ikeda.
The Hiranuma Cabinet is a Government for the con-
tinuation of the war in China, the extension of aggres-
sion in Eastern Asia and on the Pacific Ocean, the fur-
ther fascisization of the country, and the stifling of
the ever-increasing anti-war movement. Nevertheless,
Hiranuna is confronted by the same unsolved problems
on which his predecessor broke his teeth.
V. Curly
Washington, D.c.,
January 7, 1939.

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure no. 2 to despatch no. 2020
dated JAN 1 8 1939 from the American
Embassy, Moscow, U.S.S.R., on the
subject of the reorganization of
the Japanese Government.
SOURCE: Krasnaya Zvezda, Moscow, January 9,
1939.
(Organ of the People's Commissariat
of Defense.)
TRANSLATION.
REORGANIZATIOI OF THE JAPANESE GOVERNMENT.
The governmental crisis in Japan represents the out-
ward expression of the extraordinary difficulties which
confront Japanese inperialism as a result of the adven-
turous policy of the military government headed by Prince
Konoye. The Hiranuma Cabinet is composed almost entirely
of former ministers and in Japanese circles it is con-
sidered as a "reconstructed" Konoye Cabinet. The new
Governrent will undoubtedly continue the same policy of
a ression abroad and of suppression of the toilers within
the country.
The fact that representatives of the upper bour-
geoisie - Maeda of the Seiukai Party and Sakurautsi of
the Minseito Party - entered the Government will only
temporarily lessen the conflict, which is going on in
the extreme rightist camp and will in no manner cure
Japanese economy of its many ailments. On the contrary,
the policy of the new Government promises to sharpen the
relations of Japan with the other Governments whose in-
terests in China are being trampled upon by the Japanese
militarists. Confirmation of this is to be seen in the
"assent" of Hiranuma to satisfy the demands of War Min-
ister Itagaki and Naval Minister Yonai to extend military
operations in China, in order to "achieve the established
aims" and to strengthen Japanese armaments radically
"in

=== Page 25 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
"in view of the position of England, the U.S.A. and the
U.S.S.R.".
A year and a half of the predatory war in China has
ruined Japanese economy. A further continuation of the
war, which is swallowing up 20 million yen each day, may
soon lead Japan to financial crises. The first year of
the war cost Japan 8 billion yen. The Japanese Government
in order to extract such a huge sum from the national
economy, brought into bein: a number of extraordinary
laws for the "mobiliation of the country" placing under
the control of the military the greater part of the na-
tional economy. For the same purpose ar extensive of-
fensive was undertaken again-t the toilers.  12-14 hour
working day was introduced. Real wages were sharply
lowered as a result of the extr:ordinary price increases
on essential commodities.
The Konoye Govern ent at the end of December de-
cided to mobilize all the econo: c resources of Japan.
That decision envisaged the giving to the Government of
the richt to establish the scale of wages, the len th
of the working day, i.e., to exploit the toilers even
more. In addition, the Government obtained the right
to requisition stores of raw materials and manufactured
goods, factories, fields and in general "everything neces-
sary for the realization of the national policy". The
Government also made attempts to restrict the profits
of the industrialists by means of establishing a defi-
nite limit, above which dividends would be prohibited.
These measures of the Government provoked the opposition
of the industrial and financial circles; between them
and the Government there arose sharp differences.
Undoubtodly

=== Page 26 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Undoubtedly, the most serious was the disagreement
over the direction of future policy in China. Japanese
agcression had already brought on a deterioration of
Japan's relations with England and with the U.S.A., which,
although as yet hesitantly, have nevertheless begun to
take some sort of counter-measures. Among these measures
are the English and American credits to China. The Japan-
ese Government could not conceal from itself that the
granting to China of an American loan of 25 million dollars
bore evidence of the growing movement in the U.S.A. against
Japanese agcression. Roosevelt's message to Congress
serves as confirmation of this. Roosevelt states that
the U.S.A. rust stand resolutely on the side of joint
strug le with other countries againet the aggression of
Fascist powers.
The croups of the Japanese rulin circles which are
interested in trade with the U.S.A. and England opnosed
a deterioration in relations with these countries. It
is well known that the former Minister for Foreign Affairs,
Ugaki, resigned his post in September of last year because
he objected to a continuation of the present course of
foreign policy, which is placing Japan in a worse posi-
tion. The Fascist military and the magnates of Japanese
capitalism, however, not wishing to lag behind their
partners in the "Berlin-Rome-Tokyo axis", demanded an
extension of aggression, at whatever cost.
Konoye, finding himself under a double pressure,
attempted to solve all his difficulties by a quick end-
ing of the war in China. He, just as many other repre-
sentatives of the more far-seeing Japanese circles, un-
derstood that the prolongation of the Chinese war was a
great

=== Page 27 === (confidence 0.98)
-4 -
great threat to Japan. Konoye's last attempt, directed
toward keeping himself in power and freeing himself from
many difficulties, wa? the Japanese Government's declara-
tion of December 22 concerning the conditions for peace
with China. That was a trial balloon intended to be
grasped by the rightist groups of the Kuomintang. Konoye
thought that Wan Chin -wei and his supporters, - Japanese
agents, - represented a real power in China and would
be able to obtain the capitulation of the Chinese Gov-
erninent.
The Chinese Government rejected Konoye's terns.
The Chinese people proved to be more united than the
Jupanese premier had supposed and the treachery of Wang
Chin -wei in nowise weakened the deterrination of the
Chinese people to continue the strug le until final vic-
tory. On the contrary, the exposin of Wang Ching-wei
showed everyone, including the Japanese, that the capit-
ulating croup in China is weal: and doomed to failure.
Wang Chinc-wei failed, and after him Konoye also fell.
Hiranuma is a typical representative of the Japan-
ese bureaucracy, connected vith powerful concerns and
banks nd through the Fascist organizations of the type
of the "Kokuhonsha" headed by him, also with the mili-
tary, whose protegé he is. Hiranuma himself is, as it
were, the expression of the compromise as a result of
which the present Government was formed. Hiranuma brought
Maeda and Saluratsi into the Govern ent, - representa-
tives of those bour eois landlord groups which have been
waging a steady fight against the extraordinary measures
of the military, who are encroaching on their interests.
Those

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
Those are the groups of the rightist camp which fear
that their interests will suffer from any deterioration
in the international position of Japan.
The attempts of the military to create a single
governmental party have failed. Now the military is
seeking to create an appearance of "unity" by bringing
into the Government the representatives of the princi-
pal groups of the rulin classes. But all this does not
remove the reasons for the difference, and therefore the
first intensification of the difficulties will lead to
a sharpening of the internal strug le among Japan's
ruling classes. nd many such difficulties will occur.
The internal political set-up has deteriorated and the
war in China has entered a new stage, even more burden-
some for Japanese imperialism.
M. Volkov.
JPMM/hla

Tokyo's despatch no. 3600 of January 13,
1939, entitled "Japanese Cabinet Change,
January 4-5, 1939", after 11sting the
personnel of the outgoing and incoming
cabinets, reports the reason for the estab-
lishment of a new cabinet, describes the
character of the new Premier, and reports
the increased possibility of a war between
Japan and Russia 1n 1939.
The Embassy reports that Admiral
Suetsugu, Home Minister in the Konoe Cabinet,
1s the outstanding figure missing in the new
cabinet. The Embassy does not regard the
failure of the new cabinet to include former
Finance inister Ikeda as significant because
the two men who take over his offices
(Ministry of Finance and Ministry of Commerce
and Industry) can be considered supporters
of his policies.(it would seem to the writer
of this summery of the despatch that the
Embassy underestimates the loss to the
Government of Mr. Ikeda, as it is doubtful
that Mr. Ikeda's successors have the ability
and the prestige of Mr. Ikeda. This is also
the view of the Commercial Attaché at Tokyo.)
The Embessy states that inclusion in the new
cabinet of the representatives of the
Seiyuka1 and of the Minseito gives the
political

=== Page 33 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
political parties merely nominal representa-
tion, neither of the representatives being
in the ective inner cabinet membership.
The Embassy 1s convinced that the
explanation of the resignation of the Konoe
Cabinet lies in Admiral Suetsugu, who was
too insistent upon several changes being
made, including greater control of the press,
enhanced police powers, and steps toward a
one-party system. The Embassy states that
Prince Konoe, probably because of a desire to
be relieved of office, took this discord
created by Suetsugu as cause for resignation.
The Embassy also believes that another cause
for the change in government was, as commonly
steted, to the effect thet the end of one
stage in the Sino-Japanese hostilities having
arrived, it was desirable to have a new
leader, one relatively freeh in whom the
public could impose confidence.
The Embassy is evidently of the opinion
that no sharp change of policy will take
place as & result of the cabinet change. The
Embaesy believes, however, that as a result
of the chenge the possibilities of a major
conflict between the Soviet Union and Japan
are enhanced for the following reasons:
(a) The new premier, Baron Hiranuma,
as e result of his long association with
Japanese reactionaries and nationalists,
some

=== Page 34 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
some of whom favor war with the Soviet Union,
will find it more difficult to say no to
advocetes of war than did his predecessors
in that office, if and when those supporters
come to desire an 1mmediate war against the
Soviet Union; (b) the Japanese objective of
ejecting occidental political 1nfluence from
eastern Asia is now blocked with increasing
prominence by the Soviet Union; (c) Japan
is in a better position at present for war
because China is not the military threat
which it was one or two years ago; (d) the
results of the Munich conference have led
Japan to believe that no obstaclee will be
interposed against German pressure on the
Soviet Union; and (e) Japan considers the
Soviet Union at the present time internally
weakened and externally in a position of
singular isolation. The Embassy further
states that these five reasons are in addition
to the standing arguments for such a conflict,
namely, thet Japan cannot tolerate the air
menace of a hostile Vladivostock, that there
is no prospect of a peaceful solution of the
1mportant Siberian fisheries question in a
manner permanently satisfactory to Japan,
that Soviet influence in China hinders Japan's
policy there, and that the repeatedly
strengthened anti-Comintern pact must sooner
or later lead to wer between Japan and Russia.

of eny weakening of the Japanese military
strength in China proper.)

In the last week of December there appeared in the

The subject reached its least indirect expression in a very

superfluous; but that, if such developments are inescapable,

Tokyo'ε 3638 of January 27, 1938, en-
closes translations of addresses of the Prime
Minister and the Minister for Foreign Affairs
at the opening of the Seventy-fourth Diet.
Ambassador Grew comments that Baron Hiranuma,
in conformity with recently established
precedent in official Japanese statements,
confined himself to vague generalities and
that the Premier's statement that economic
control is to be strengthened by other
measures "of a permanent and constructive
character" envisages further implementation
of the National General Mobilization Law.
Mr. Grew reports that press reaction to the
Prime Minister'ε address was noticeably cool,
criticism having been made that nothing
original or suggestive for dealing with the
present situation could be found in the
address.
The same general criticisms were made
concerning Mr. Arita's address  that it
contained nothing new. Mr. Arita stated,
"But there is no doubt that the orincipal
causes (of the present international
anxiety and unrest) lie essentially in ef-
forts to maintain a status quo which is
actually not in accordance with justice
and attemots made from selfish motives to
stifle the development and orogress of newly
risen powers." This statement was given
hearty approval by all papers, although many
felt that Arita had not gone far enough
toward

=== Page 49 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE

-2-
toward stiffering Japan's attitude toward
third countries. The oroposal further to
strengthen the Anti-Comintern Pact was
"armly welcomed and in some quarters a
military alliance was reported imminent or
was urged as a definite progressive step.
It vill be recalled trat it was re-
ported in the oress that the Prime Minister
had said that Japan would have no alternative
but to exterminate those who opposed Japan.
The im lication in the press reports was that
this statement was aimed at foreign powers.
From the context of the speech, however, it
would seem that Baron Hiranuma aimed his re-
marks at the Chinese who continue to oppose
Japan.
Mr. Arita's address in large part con-
sisted of a reaffirmation of the Japanese
Government's statements of November 2 and
December 22, 1338. There are quoted below
some of the highlights in the adaress:

the realization of those ends.

Contrary to predictions in the vernacular press that
the Premier's address would contain expressions of the
basic policies of the new cabinet and would set forth
concrete measures for the fulfillment of Japan's anncunced
objectives, Baron Hiranuma, in conformity with recently
CeF
established precedent in official Japanese statements, con-
fined himself to vague ceneralities. He urged "enhancement
of the national spirit on the basis of united service to the
Throne", and en hasized that the policies and objectives of
the preceding cabinet will be pursuec.
This program will,
the Premier said, re uire continuance of economic control
measures, but ne sinificently added that these are to be
strengthened by the enforcement "of other measures which
are of a permanent and constructive character". This en-
visages further implementation of the National General
Mobilization Law.
Press reaction to the Prime Minister's address was
noticeably cool. The following translations of excerpts
from the editorials in the leading vernacular papers are
believed to be representative of tne attitude taken by the
press:
NICHI NICHI - January 22, 1939.
...it represented nothing more than a
continuation of the policies of the preceding cabinet,
and it is greatly regretted that nothing original or
suggestive for dealing with the present situation
could be found......
The very point which we wished most to hear
discussed by Prime Minister Hiranuma, as the leader
of the cabinet, was what definite form his political
policies for dealing with actual political affairs
would assume.....
However,

=== Page 54 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
However, we believe the real problem is not
external but rather is internal; if there be
harmony within the nation, any external problem no
matter how difficult can be successfully solveu......
YOMIURI - January 22, 1939.
.....The Premier's aadress was from this
viewpoint (clarification of political policies) too
abstract in its hraseolory, but it did show recognition
of the fact that politics have become the outstanding
problem in the nrecent situation. (Political affairs
are more important than military at the present stage
of the China incident).
ASAhI - January 22, 1939.
....Je are afraid that the Premier's
speech which dia not present concrete measures to
the people........may not exceed a simple bureau-
cratic instruction.
CHUGAI S.OGYO -
(The addresses)..........were nothing but reports of
past uvents and were abstract and did not extend to
a ciscussion or concrete nolitical policies.
The same veneral criticisms were made concerning r.
Arita's address in that it "contained nothing new". The
press, however, was very firm in its support of Japan's
forein policy. r. Arita stated, "But there is no doubt
that the principal causes (of the present international
anxiety and unrest) lie essentially in efforts to maintain
a status quo which is actually not in accordance with
justice and in atteapts mace from selfish motives to
stifle the development and progress of newly risen powers."
This statement was iven hearty approval by all papers
and many, such as the NICHI NICHI anā the KOKUMIN, felt
that the Minister for Foreign Affairs had not gone far
enough toward stiffening of Japan's attitude toward third
countries. The press also urged Mr. Arita to take steps
to

=== Page 55 === (confidence 0.97)
-4-
to bring about an understanding on the part of the
United States and Great Britain of the "true situation
in East Asia." The proposals to further strengthen
the anti-Comintern pact were warmly welcomed and in some
quarters a military alliance was reported imminent or
was urged as a definite progressive step.
Neither of these speeches brought out new problems
or facts which have not been known heretofore. However,
the general disappointment at the lack of definiteness
in the Premier's address and the failure to deal con-
cretely with political problems, and the general strong
tone of the press with regard to foreign relations, are
to be noted.

February 23, 1939.
Reference Tokyo's 0. 3640 of January 30,
1939, entitled "Further Implenentation of"the
National General ..obilization Law".
The Japanese Government has issued an ordi-
nence under the General ..obilization Law requir-
ing the registration of all skilled workers,
technicians, mechanics, holders of certain ii-
censes and graduates and students of certain
chools. The registration is to be completed
by March 31 and is expected to reach some five
million males in about l34 kinds of work.
The Cabinet lannine Board has been streng-
thened and reorganized. .r. Kazuo Aoki, former-
ly Vice :resident, has been made iresident. The
Cabinet llanning Board is the administrative
autrority for national general mobilization (it
aypears to be the principal planning agency of
the Japanese Government).
During the fall of 1938 there was
agitation for the issuance of un order under
the General obilization Law limiting dividends
but due to the orrosition of Finance Minister
Ikeda and business circles this project was
abandon-d. Instead a very mild statement was
issued to tne effect that firms now paying 10%
per annum should not increase their payments.
In commenting upon the increasing regimen-
tation in Japan the Ambassador. states that
"the question constantly in our minds is
whether

=== Page 63 === (confidence 0.96)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
whether the objectives sought are fundamentally
congenial to the social and political genius of
the Japanese people". The uestion remains
unanswered: the report states that Japanese
themselves are divided on this point but that
they are unanimous in their opinion that there
can be no let-up in regimentation at least
until after the conflict with China has been
settled. The Embassy concurs in this view.

Nith reference to my despatch No. 3380, November 1,
1938, and to others on the subject of general mobilization
1/
in Japan, I have the honor to forward herewith the Embassy
translation of a recent Imperial Ordinance instituting a
system of re istration of the vocational abilities of Japan-
F/FG
ese subjects.
This

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.94)
-2-
This ordinance which is an allocation of broad
authority, on the basis of Article 21 of the National
General Mobilization Law, to the Welfare Minister, pre-
fectural governors, chiefs of government employment
agencies, and other local officials, was supplemented
by ministerial ordinances and notifications issued by
the Welfare Ministry on January 18, and effeotive from
January 20, 1939. These set forth fully the detailed
regulations for the application of the original ordinance.
It will be recalled that members of the medical pro-
fession (doctors, dentists, pharmicists, nurses, etc.)
were required to register under an ordinance issued
last year. The present regulations are applicable to all
skilled workers, technicians, mechanics, holders of
certain licenses, graduates of certain schools and
students who have completed certain courses. The follow-
ing is a brief summary of the more important persons
required to register:
Skilled laborers in the machine tool industry.

=== Page 66 === (confidence 1.00)
-3-
Each person required to register is to be olassified
as possessing a certain standard of technical ability. Two
or three degrees of skill are given for each category, and
those registered are to be rated after investigation by
competent authorities.
Notifications of the regulations issued by the Welfare
Ministry concerning the students and graduates of certain
courses and colleges may be summarized as follows:
1. Courses in Engineering Departments of all
Universities.
2.
Technical colleges.
3.
Foreign language schools.
4.
All vocational schools.
5.
Courses in architecture, and Russian, Spanish,
and Mongolian languages.
Government technical training institutes.
7. All who have passed official examinations
in air navigation, electricity, gas, etc.,
or who have graduated from technical high-
schools.
The list of vocations for which registration is re-
quired is most comprehensive and corresponds to the list
of workers and technicians which was summarized above.
The individual and his employer are jointly responsible
for effecting registration and for keeping the registered
data current. Upon completion of registration each in-
dividual is to be given a card showing his vocational
status and rated degree of ability.
An article published in the official magazine SHUHO,
January 18, 1939, by the Welfare Ministry, set forth the
necessity for vocational registration of the people. In
brief, it is said to be necessary, so that the Government
may be well informed of the capabilities of each individual
in order most efficiently to employ and utilize the entire
nation's strength in any emergency. The advantages of such
a

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.97)
-4-
a system, even in ordinary times, for the control of tech-
nolocical unemploy. nt and the tiversion of laborers from
ne pursuit to another are obvious. It is further stated
that the difficulty in jud ing various degrees of ability
is well reco nized and that local officials are accordingly
irected and enpowered to make extensive investigations
including inspection or Norx performed in local factories.
This rating of indiviuual ability should serve as a spur
to inciviuual elrort to raise one's own rating to the top
raik. Coupled with a natiennl system of Governnent employ-
ent agencies this netionl reci tration represents a great
undertaking in sociel pl n in and should provide a wealth
of stoti tics vitolly necessary to a planned economy. It
is reported that regi trotion, vhici. began January 0, is
to be com leted by eren oi, l9s9, enc is expected toreach
some five million males in about lo4 kinds of work.
In connection with the implementat on of the ational
Generel Mobilization Law in Jaoan it should be noted that
for some time, particularly since the early part of Novem-
ber of last year, considerable discussion has appeared in
the press conceruin the Govermment's intention to expand and
strengthen the Cabinet Planning Board and to make of this
Board the aumini trative authority for national general
mobilization. It will, of course, be remembered that it
was the Planning Board which drafted the original law.
It was reported that the plan was to make the Cabinet
Planning Board the counterpart in internal affairs of
the KOA-IN (Asia Promotion Board). A later announcement
was made to the effect that the Planning Board is to become
the

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
the central organ for the coordination and adjustment of
political, econo ic, and cultural affairs of Japan,
"Manchukuo", and China and the administrative body for

of the Konoye Cabinet, Mr. Taki, President of the Planning
Board also resigned. Durin: the month of December practically
nothing further had been sid concerning the reorganization
of the Plannin Board, but following r. Tuki's resignation
in the early part of January reports of such reorganization
a ain became prominent in the press. It was stated that

plan and was recontmenuin General Kuniaki Koiso, former
Juartermaster General or the Arny, for the Presidency. It
was also suggested that the Fresident of the Planning Board
be given the rank or & ini ter of State.

ay the press gave the followin outline which was said

PA/H:
Tokyo's despetch no. 3683 of February 15,
1939, entitled "Asia Promotion Board (KOA-IN)
reports trenchant criticism in the House of
Peers of the divislon of control over Japan's
China policy which has resulted from the
esteblishment of the Asia Promotion Board,
es that Boerd controls the political,
economic and cultural relations of Japan and
Chine while the Ministry for Foreign Affairs
controls diplomacy in regerd thereto. The
critic in the House of Peers in addition to
pointing out the resultant confusion urged
thet Japen immediately undertake constructive
works in China under the leadership of the
most highly able persons. Premier Hiranuma
replied that the activities of the Board and
of the Ministry would on occasion coincide
but that the Foreign Minister, being con-
currently a vice president of the Board,
would act as liaison agent. The Embassy
believes that Hirenuma's replies could
scarcely heve been satisfactory to the critic.
The replies indicated thet the problem still
exiets of the control of Japanese authorities
in Chine by Japanese authorities in Japan.
In another debate it was stated thet agencies
of the Board would be established in Peiping,
Tientsin, Suiyuan, and Shangha1 but that
no definite plan had as yet been formulated.

ASSISIANI SECRETARY
ir:

OF STATE
It will be recalled that in my despatch No. 3320,
FTLED
CC
ctober 7, 1938, concerning the resignation of General
gaki, former Minister for Foreign Affairs, I had the
onor to report that one of the more important causes for
General Ugaki's resignation was his inability to accept a
F/A
position in which he, as the Minister of State for Foreign
Affairs, would be deprived of the prerogatives and functions
inherent

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
inherent in that office. I have in various despatches
reported to the epartment the progress of the movement
to establish an authority for the control of Sino-Japanese
relations. Reference is made in particular to my despatch
No. 3549, December 23, 198, with which the ordinances
setting forth the organization of the Asia Promotion Board
were forwarded.
r. Hashio Yeava, a former member of the Foreign
Service of Japan and at one time Chief of the Treaty
Bureau of the Foreign Office, and later Director of the
Bureau of Legislation of the Cabinet, was the first in-
terpellator in the House of Peers following the official
addresses by the various ministers of State. Mr. Yamakawa,
in view of his broad experiences and clear understanding
of the problem, was able to point out with remarkable
clarity the somewhat aunbiguous situation in which the
Ministry for Foreign Affairs has been placed with the
setting up of a board w. icn is to control the political,
economic, and cultural relations between Japan and China
but which is, according to the articles of its organization,
not to administer diplomatic affairs. With considerable
frankness, Mr. Yamakawa expressed belief that it would
be far more politic to have the entire Ministry of Foreign
Affairs absorbed by the Asia Promotion Board than to
create a situation in which relations with China, the very
heart, he said, of all of Japan's foreign relations, would
be under a divided supervision. Such a situation, he
pointed out, was intolerable and would not make for unity
in Japan's diplomatic relations.
Mr.

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Mr. Yamakawa, however, did not confine himself to
criticism but, acce, ting the Asia Promotion Board as a
fait accompli, questioned the Government on its specific
plans for the prosecution of Sino-Japanese relations. He
clearly indicated the confusion with regard to this vital
problem which exists within Government circles and stated
that in his opinion if this new organization is necessary
for the realization of Japan's hin policy, such an
organization should be utilized in the most effective
and practical manner.
. He urged further that it was of
the utmost importance for Japan to undertake imnediately
constructive works in China and that to do this individuals
of the hi hest ability shoulā be selected to carry out
these long-term responsibilities.
Premier Hiranuma in a subse uent session undertook
to reply to these interpellations. His answer, which
must have been unsatisfactory and not informative to
Mr. Yamakawa, merely emphasized that there does exist
an anomaly in the administration of Japan's foreign
relations. The Prime Minister said that the Asia Pro-
motion Board was specifically prohibited from administer-
ing any diplomatic affairs either in the reletions with
China or with any other country. He did, however, admit
that the activities of t is new Board and of the Ministry
of Foreign Affairs would on occasion coincide. In this
instance, Baron Hiranuma stated, the Minister for Foreign
Affairs, being concurrently a Vice President of the Asia
Promotinn Board, would act as liaison agent and therefore
would

=== Page 90 === (confidence 0.97)
. .
-4-
would prevent the occurrence of any friction between
the two offices. Tne Prime Miister elso said that
the Government would carefully deliberate on pertinent
neasures to prevent the rise of any discord between
the "central atthorities and the authorities at the
front". From this answer it is obvious that there
still exists the problen of the control of Japanese
authorities in China by Japanese authorities in Japan.
Nor has the question, wlich I set forth in my despatch
No. 3549, December 23, 1938, as to the possible extent

Sir:
I had the honor to present in my 44, January 24,
7 p.m. a report in outline of the discussions which
took place in the Lower House of the Diet with regard
to Janan's policy vis-à-vis China and her relations
with the Powers s they have been affected by con-
ditions now prevailing in China.
On January 2l, immediately after the principal
F
ministers completed their annual addresses to the
A
House, a lengthy interpellation on various aspects
of

=== Page 96 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
of Japan's foreign relations was made by Mr. Ogawa,
a member of the Minseito and formerly Minister of
Commerce and Industry. The character of the specifio
0,Gdto
questions put to the Minister for Foreign Affairs by
this member is indicated in Mr. Arita's reply, a trans-
1/
lation of which is enclosed, and a detailed examination
of the views expressed by the member would not be pro-
fitable; but the fact is of interest that a speech
breathing chauvinism was apparently highly pleasing to
the house, as indicated by the frequency with which
Mr. Ogawa's remarks were interrupted by applause.
Perhaps the most characteristic statement reflective
of his disapproval of the efforts of Mr. Arita to
conciliate the Powers was the following:
"The uestion which many of us would like
to rut to the Government is this, How can the
United States and Great Britain be made to have
a correct appreciation of the new order in ast
Asia? The Foreign Minister states that he is
resorting to reasoned discussion with these
Powers. Debate is necessary, of course, and is
one method: but the point which I wish to stress
is that another method and the best method of
inducing a change of attitude on the part of
these Powers is to confront them with facts."
Mr. Arita's reply deserves to be read in full.
He was confronted by a house which, although not de-
finitely hostile, was obviously not in a mood to listen
sympathetically to a plea for conciliation with the
Powers.

=== Page 97 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
Powers. His own views with regard to the need for such
conciliation were not expressed until January 23, when,
goaded by jibes, he declared, "Of course we have taken
a firm resolve to carry out our national policies, but I
see no need for creating unnecessary friction with the
Powers". His attempt in the reply to Mr. Ogawa to steer
a course through these difficulties - to emphasize the
determination of Japan to establish the "new order in
East Asia" and to provide a new definition of the principle
of equal opportunity calculated to be acceptable to the
Powers - was one to which he repeatedly referred in sub-
secuent discussions. The persistence with which he clung
to that statement and his evoidance of expansion or modi-

foreign relations of interest or of substantial significance.
In my telegram under reference, I made brief mention

being imposed upon Japan by the Tnited tates and Great
Britain. The brief statement on this subject of the Prime
Minister, quoted in my telegram, along with the expressed
sentiments of members, are along lines which might have
been expected. Whether Japan would display an accommodating
attitude if actually confronted by sanctions is a question
which cannot be conclusively answered before Japan is put
to the test.

Translation.
PROCEEDI CS OF THE LOWER HCUSE ON JANUARY 21, 1939.
(Tokyo OFFICIAL GAZETTE,January 22, 1939)
The  inister for Foreign Affairs(Mr. Arita), re-
plying to uestions by Mr. Ogawa, said:
The first question put by Mr. Ogawa was, "How do
we propose to deal with the anti-Japanese feeling which
now pervades China" Due to the anti-Japanese educa-
tional campaign carried out over a period of years
by Chiang Kai-shek, the feeling of animosity against
Japan prevails extremely widely throughout China and
is very deeply rooted. To change that feeling is, in
my opinion, a very great undertaking. As was just
stated by the Prime inister, the first thing necessary
to eliminate that feeling is to lay down various cul-
tural projects. Japan's moral mission cannot be accom-
plished unless there is entire consistency between what
she says and what she does. We must divest ourselves
of any sense of superiority which we may have over the
Chinese and equality must be made the essence of our
position. Further, with regard to the relations whioh
must be maintained with the new government about to be
formed in China, it is very necessary that we should
not

=== Page 99 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
not adopt an attitude of leadership. What is also very
important is that all Japanese, whether government offi-
cials or civ lians, should hereafter be prepared to coop-
erate with China, and, in the spirit of the statement
issued by Prine Konoye on December 22, to do everything
possible to place on a stable basis the relation between
the Japanese Government and the new government to be formed
in China. It is only by conforming to that attitude of
cooperation that we can com letely deliver China from
the state of being a semi-colonial nation. So long as we
base our policies and attitude on that spirit, I am con-
fident that we will be able to eliminate Chinese ani-
mosity a ainst Japan.
The next question was whether e new central govern-
ment should be set up in China for the purpose of weaken-
ing the anti-Jopanese Regime. It is true that a new govern-
ment would serve the purpose of weakening ti: anti-Japa-
nese Regime, but a new government is also needed in order
to provide a basis for establishing new relations between
China and Japan. The Japanese Government expects that
such a new government will shortly be established, and
various reports indicate that the time for the setting up
of such a government is approaching. It is, however, im-
possible to say whether that new government will develop
out of the now existing joint committees, or whether
something new will develop, but in either event that
will be a matter, in my opinion, for the Chinese themselves
to

=== Page 100 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
to decide:this is not a question where Japanese directive
would be proper. What we anticipate is that the new govern-
ment develop in & normal and natural method.
Some reference
was made to Wang Ching-wei. 'r. Wang has left Chungking
and is now at Hanoi, from which place he is addressing
telegrams to Chiang Yai-shek emphasizing the need for
peece. These developments which have had strong reper-
cussions in China, have also had some effect in third
countries. However, the Japanese Government has no in-
formation other than that which has appeared in the press
with regard to the future intentions of I'r. Wang. It is
nevertheless the feeling of the Jananese Government that
these developments have had extremely importent effects;
that they may constitute one of the reasons for the future
cleavage within the nationalist government of China; and
that they may be one of the causes for the future collapse
of that government.
I shall next take up the cuestion of the relationship
between the Far Fastern economic bloc and the princinle of
equality of opportunity. To set up a new order in Asia
is a policy of Jopan. The phrase "Far Eastern bloc" is
sometimes used to describe the very close relationship
which is contemplated for Japan, !enchukuo, and China,
but the use of this phrase to describe this economic
relationship between the three countries in the Far East
is capable of giving rise to much misunderstanding. The
word "bloc" implies the exclusion of others, and there
appears to be some disposition to assume that that is
our

=== Page 101 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
our intention, and for that reason we are refraining from
the use of the phrase "economic bloc". It is true that
when Japan sets up the close economic relationship be-
tween Japan, Manchukuo and China, there will inevitably
appear a gap between that association and the principle
of the ecuality of opportunity as hitherto understood.
But, Japan is impelled from the point of view of national
defense and economic self sufficiency, to move toward
setting up what r. Ogawa designates as "an economic bloc".
It is well known that Great Britain and the United
States have protested.
It is the desire of the Imperial
Government to avoid so far as possible engaging in profit-
less controversy by exchange of diplomatic documents. We
have fully explsined to the ambassadors of the concerned
countries what Japan wants and wiat Jepan has in mind, but
these points have not been adequately understood. As you
are aware, communications were received from the United
States on December 31, and from Great Britain on January 14,
and quite recently one from France, affirming that Japan's
contentions could not be admitted.
With regard to the
reasons and circumstances which prevent the United States
and Great Britain from recognizing Japan's contentions,
emphasis is laid on the fact that the new economic asso-
ciation between Japan, Manchukuo, and China would result in
completely eliminating from China the economic activities of
nationals of these and other third countries. Their
position arises from this apprehension. What causes them
to have this fear is that, among the various incidents which
have

=== Page 102 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
have arisen in China involving third countries there
have been a nunber which have thus far not been capable
of settle ent. Further, in connection with the present
hostilities in China, various anomalous and extraordinary
conditions have obtained. It is argued that, as indicated
by these conditions, there is a gap between what Japan
does and what she states she will do, and that in actual
fact what is contemplated is the elimination from China
of the econonic activities of British, American, and other
foreign nationals. As a matter of fact a large number of
these incidents have not been settled because they were
due to military necessity or,because of military oper-
ations, investigations could not be carried out. If there
had been no military necessity, or if it had been possible
to carry out investigations, tiese are incidents which
normally would have been settled. In other instances,
Jepenese actions have siven rise to widespread suspicion,
but as these actions were unavoidably caused by the ab-
normal conditions now prevailing or were military in
character, most of these actions or conditions will be
cleared up so soon as the extraordinary situation is
corrected.
Although these circumstances have been ex-
plained to the American and British Ambassadors, they
do not seem to be sufficiently appreciated. As you are no
doubt aware the recent American and British notes take
a theoretical position and oppose the Japanese con-
tentions all along the line, but they add that as treaties
are not necessarily eternal, there is disposition on the
part of the respective countries to discuss, under special
circumstances, modification of treaties. It would be
appropriate

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
appropriate, in my opinion, to regard this offer as
appreciation, up to a certain point, of the Japanese
contention that unere is a new order in East Asia. As
I have just stated, we have explained on repeated occa-
sions to Great Britain and the United States, and other
countries that close economic cooperation between Japan,
Manchukuo, and China is absolutely essential and that
some limitation must inevitably be imposed on the so-
called Open Door or the principle of equal opportunity,
and as occasion offers it is my intention to explain
this policy.

The thesis of this despatch does
not appear very convincing to me. Even
granting the postulate that Japan 1s
evolving toward an increasing social
control of economic life, I do not see
that the resulting increase in the
administrative tasks of the civilian
bureaucracy would necessarily or even
probably weaken the power of the mili-
tary and lead to Japan's becoming once
more a nation "with which other nations
could maintain normal political and com-
mercial relatlons." Is not the matter
of the dominance of the military caste
more related to other social and political
factors than whether the economic system
1s one of free private capitalism or
social control? That is, if the power
of the military declines, will it not
be because Japan no longer has need of
a great military force to attain certain
national objectives nor to defend
national interests against other strong
military powers.
The primary purpose of most of
Japan's social control of business has
been

=== Page 109 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON
INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC AFFAIRS
-2
been to increase military effeotiveness,
and the self-cufficient empire in Asia
at which she appears to be aiming is
primarily a military concept and objec-
tive that must be attained and defended
by military power. The mere fact that
economic control may shift from private
business to a Governmental bureaucracy
would not necessarily weaken the suprem-
acy of the military in the Government
itself. In fact, I should think that
it might even facilitate the control of
all aspects of national life by the
military. It is difficult for me to
believe that a powerful business class
would not offer a more effective check
on the military than a bureaucracy which
1s in complete subservience to the State
and hence to the chief political power
that controls the State.

Tokyo's 3778 of March 28, 1939, deals
with the National Mobilization Law and the

discerns in the preparations to put into
force certaln of the provisions of the law
in question, such as those in regard to
restrictions on corporation profits and price
control, an acceleration in the movement
toward state socialism. Paralleling the
preparations mentioned, a National Spiritual
Mobil1zation Committee under the chairmanship
of the Minister of Education is taking over
the leadership in the task to meet the needs
of continuing to sell to the Japanese public
the 1dea of East Asia under Japanese hegemony
with all its implicatlons of a long-pull
struggle.
The Embassy points out that, while
various administrative chenges have taken
place within the framework of the Japanese
national pol1ty, these changes do not yet in-
volve any .organic change in the structure of the
government and that it would be incorrect and
misleading to draw analogies between the
socialism evolving in Japan and the systems set
up in Germany and Italy, as the Japanese politi-
cal genius has shown itself over a period of
fifteen or more centuries in the erection of
complicated and cumbersome political systems,
largely oligarchical, of checks and balances
with no political figure arising to dominate
the

=== Page 111 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
the scene alone. There is the probebility
that the system of socialism which is evolving
will strengthen the bureaucratic character
of the Japanese Government. If this proves
to be the case,dominance of the military in
Japanese political life may be of a temporary
character destined to give way to a resurgence
of the bureaucratic elements once the gains
in China have been consolidated. In such
circumstances it may be envisaged that Japan
will again become a nation with which other

to this information the morale of the people

of labor has increased wages and has given
highly paid jobs to unskilled labor, end
amusement places are making more money.
The remeinder of the report contains
information which has in lerge measure been

The Consul General reports that emong
educated and better informed classes there is
an increase of caution mixed with disillusion-
ment because of evidence that the United
States is becoming more closely ellied with
Great Britain and France which will presunably
be to the detriment of the authoritarian
powers.
From page 17 to 22 is discussed evidence
of Japan's preparation for a long-drawn out
struggle with China and from rage 23 to the

strain under which Japan is beginning to
suffer.

The War Still Has Popular Support.
In spite of the heavy casualties suffered and the
mounting costs of the China Incident, the morale of
the people in this part of the country remains high
and their faith in their Government and their army re-
mains unshaken.
It is true that there is a certain amount of war
weariness observable among the people, but it would be
very easy to exaggerate its depths or extent. It seems
to be confined to a relatively small peroentage of the
population, that is, those whose businesses or liveli-
hood have been adversely affected by the control measures
now in foroe.
By and large the war continues to have the support
of the great majority. The general feeling is that most
of the major battles have been fought and won and that
future operations will consist largely of extermimatins
the guerrillas and policing the oceupied areas. However,
even should further heavy fighting md additiomal esenomie
restrietions be necessary, the people are ready to fellew
their leaders, ready and willing to suppert the war as
long as may be necessary.
The people have mkready made a lot of mesey eut of
the Bmergeney situatien and they hepe te make a les mere

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.97)
before it is over. They have made some sacrifices in
the way of paying increased taxes, higher priees for
food and clothing, eto., but what they have given is
far outweighed by what they have already gotten, to say
nothing of the additional benefits which they Hop to
receive. In other words, benefits, actual and anticipated,
are for the masses more important than the relatively
minor hardships which they are called upon to bear. of
course, there is the dizzily mounting national debt,
the growing prospeots of dangerous inflation, eto.,
but the common people do not understand these things,
and hence are not worrying about them.
The capitalist class finds the war situation to
its liking because it offers it the chance of a lifetime
to make a financial killing. This is so because of the
huge sums of "easy" money being made to flow as a result
of the Government's economic and militery policies.
One of the key objectives of the Government is to make
the country as nearly self sufficient in en imduserial
sense as the availahle resources and capital will allow.
This involves a tremendous expensien of existins pre-
duetive capaeity and the establishment of numereus mew
industries, especially these dessined to preduse sm-
thetie or substitute materials.
This hage iadustrial expansios progremesta to

=== Page 132 === (confidence 0.95)
- 5 -
fiscal funds. In other cases new enterprises have
been brought into existenee by guarantees of tax
exemption, subsidies, cheap loans, ete. of course,
practically all concerns capable of produsing military
supplies are receiving big army contracts on lueretive
terms. Even those firms not producing supplies of
direct military value find the present situation ex-
ceedingly profitable, because the Government's poliey

Food supplies continue to be plentiful and reasonably
priced and the rentals charged for working men's houses
have remained elmost stationary. The quality of textile
materials and clothing has deteriorated, but there is
still ample material to go around. Consequently the
workers' spending power is greater than ever before.
As a result, movie theaters and restaurants are filled
to capacity and the consumption of beer and sake has
risen appreciably. It is said that the number of
geishe end also the number of women in the licensed
houses is at an all time high mark.

Strens Talk From Weshington Produces Increaced Cautiom
Hapan。
The popularity of the China venture remains
practically as strong today as it ever was, but among
the edueated and better informed classes an attitude
of imreased caution, mixed with disillusiemment, has
lately developed. The cause of this is the growing
hostility of the American Government and people to the
dictator regimes of Europe, that is to say, to Jmpan's
closest friends and presumptive allies.
After nearly two years of war this country has
beeome more or less inured to the widespread criticism
leveled against it on moral grounds, and also the fre-
quent protests from Great Britain, Franee, and the
United States over the repeated violations of tmir
treaty rights in China. These protests have caused
annoyance and resentment, but they have newer intimidated
this sountry or caused it to swerve from its objeetives.
Stronger measures than these would be required, and this
country has figured right alons that forke would not be
applied. It would compromise on non-essentials where
it mast, but it would still be able to prusue its china
pregrem without estive interference frem third pewers.
Opinien here has been right aleng to the effeet thes
the United states weuld be deriaitely line to r

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.96)
from taking any strong action in the Far East. The
Munieh agreement of last fall merely served to stremgthes
the sonvi etion that neither Franee nor Britain weuld
sight.
The surrender of the European demoeracies was a
keen disappointment to most of the world, Japan ineluded.
while most countries regretted the surrender as a patent
manifestation that the demooracies were afraid of the
totalitarian States, Japan regretted the surrender from
a totally different angle. People here had been follow-
ing the crisis in Europe with keen interest, and many had
hoped that they might result, not in peace, but in a
general European war. They remembered how handeomely
this country had fared during the World War and they
figured that another general conflict at this time would
be exeeedingly profitable, especially if this country
could remain on the side-lines. When the European situa-

material, production and consumption. Hence if any

May 4, 1939.
Tokyo's despatch no. 38l5 of April 11,
1939, entitled "Appointment of Full-Time
Minister for Overseas Affairs and minister
of Communications", reports the appointment
of General nuniaki hoiso as minister for
Overseas affairs and of Mr. Harunichi Tanabe
as .inister of Communications, posts which
had heretofore been held concurrently by
other Cabinet inisters. The Embassy states
that (1)  inclusion in the Cabinet of Gen-
eral noiso, who is one of the most outspoken
of the politically-minded group in the Army
and who has been closely associated with the
single-party movement in Japan, may betoken
an intention to reform the structure of the
Government or, more probably, may be for the
purpose of aliaying criticism of reactionary
reform groups for the 1remier's failure to
live uj to commitments in regard to struc-
tural reform; (2) by the appointment of Mr.
Tanabe the 1renier brings into the Cabinet
one of his faithful followers of many years'
standing to support him in carrying out his
aims; and (3) these two appointments induct
into the Cabinet two able men, relieve other
Cabinet Ministers of some of their heavy work,
and strengthen a Cabinet which has lacked
brilliance.
This is a competent despatch drafted by
Frank Schuler.

Sir:
I have the honor to report that the appointments of
Affairs, and Mr. Harumichi Tanabe as Minister of Communi-
cations were gazetted under date of April 7, 1939. The
Overseas affairs portfolio has been held concurrently by
the Minister of Commerce and Industry, Mr. Yoshiaki Hatta,
and the Communications portfolio, by Minister of Justice,
Mr. Suehiko Shiono, since the inception of the Hiranua
60
F/FG
Cabinet in January.
The

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The newly appointed Minister for Overseas Affairs,
General Koiso, has been closely connected with Japanese
expansion on the continent, having been director of the
Military Affairs Bureau of the War Ministry at the outbreak
of the Manchurien Incident in 193l. Later as Chief of Staff
of the Kwantung Army he played an important role in the
consolidation of the Army's position in Manchuria. In 1935
he became commander of the Korean Army, but was placed on
the reserve list in July, l938, reportedly at the request
of the War Minister, Generel Seishiro Itagaki, to make way
for younger men, although the rumor was current at the time
that he was thus punished for alleged mishandling of the
"Changkufeng affeir". A classmate of General Gen Sugiyama,
Conmander of the Imperial forces in North China, and of
Lieutenant General Heisuke Yanagawa, the Director of the
Asia Fromotion Board (KOA-IN), General Koiso is one of the
most outspoken of the politically-minded group in the Army
and has been closely associated with the single-party movement.
His accuaintance with the Prime Minister, Baron Hiranuma,
dates back several years to the time when both were leaders
in the Kokuhonsha, a reactionary organization with the avowed
purpose of reconstructing the government on the basis of the
"Imperial Way".
The new Minister of Communications, Mr. Harumichi Tanabe,
who relinquishes the post of Chief Secretary of the Cabinet,
has been closely connected with communication problems since
his graduation from Tokyo Imperial University in l905, having
served in the Ministry of Communications in various capacities
since that time. Crowning his career as ordinary civil
offioial

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
official he was appointed Governor of Osaka Prefecture
1n 1927.
. He was called from retirement in 1932 to the
post of frivy Councillor of "Manchukuo", which post he held
until 1937. During the Hayashi Cabinet in 1937 Mr. Tanabe
was prominently mentioned for the post of Minister of
Communications, but was, according to report, dissuaded from
accepting by the Kwantung Army which was in greater need
of his services. Mr. Tanabe is an old friend of the
Prime Minister's, and during his incumbency of the office
of Chief 3ecretary of the Cabinet he presumably strengthened
this relationship. His successor in that office, Mr. Ota,
001
is an attorney by profession, a charter member of the
Ko
Kokuhonsha, and a faithful follower of the Prime Minister,
Baron iranuma, for the past twenty years.
The appointment of a full-time Minister for Overseas
Affairs and a full-time Minister of Communications may be
regarded from the standpoint of internal politics as the
strengthening of a Cabinet which has lacked brilliance. From
the purely mechanical side the appointments will serve to
relieve the other members of the Cabinet of a considerable
burden and will also act as a gesture toward the return of
the Cabinet to normal working conditions. The present move
may also be looked upon as the determination of the Prime
Minister and his advisors to put into these two important posts
men who are thoroughly familiar with the problems Japan is
facing at the present time. General Koiso is well-acquainted
with the problem of emigration to "Manchukuo", over which his
Ministry has jurisdiction, because of his long service
1
in

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
in Manchuria and Korea. Aside from his friendship
with the Frime Minister he is said to be on good terms
sadao
with General Araki, the Education Minister, having served
OBPJV
under him as Vice Minister of War in the Inukai and Saito
1
Cabinets.: oth are proponents of the vague doctrine called
1
the Imperial Way. As an expert on communications, Mr. Tanabe
will brin to the Ministry of Conmunications the requisite
talent needed for the task of controlling electric power,
shipping, and civil aviation, and for integrating this work
with plens which have been developed for expansion on the
continent. Likevise the Prime Minister will have in one
of his most important ministries "a friend at court" who
will serve hin well in carrying out his political aims.
Some observers incline to the belief that the inclusion
of Cenerel Koiso, an outspoken reformist, into the Cabinet
betokens the deter ination of the Prime Minister, Baron
hiranuma, to undertake a general reformation of the govern-
mental structure. Offsetting tiis are the repeated
declerations of the Fremier that he will continue to respect
the present order so far as the political parties are concerned
and thet he intends to maintain the Cabinet system as at
present constituted. A more likely interpretation is that
the Prime inister, himself at one time an avowed reformist,
probably feels keenly the criticism of reform groups outside
the Cabinet that he has failed them and that they are without
adequate representation in the Government. By appointing
General Koiso he has tried to kill two birds with one stone:
remove

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.94)
-5-
remove the sting from the barbs of his critics and make
his Cabinet a more workable political instrument for the
tasks lying before it.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
U
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
May 24, 1939.
The Japanese Foreign Minister informed
Mr. Grew (1) that although Japan desires to
avoid European entangloments and to maintain
strict neutrality between the democratic and
totalitarian states apart from the specific
field of combatting communistic activities,
Japan might find it difficult to avoid involve-
ment in case the Soviet Union should become
involved in a European war and would have to
reconsider Japan's position if Britain and
France concluded a military alliance with the
Soviet Union and (2) that in the event of a
European war in which the United States became
involved the position which the Soviet Union
might take might conceivably decide whether
peace could be maintained between Japan and
the United States.
In reply to Mr. Grew's questions in re-
gard to American rights and interests, Arita's
replies were vague but he indicated (a) that
there was no intention to limit American trade
beyond the requirements of military necessity,
(b) that certain restrictive measures were of
a temporary character while others, such as
exchange control in north China might be perma-
nent, (c) that Japan had no intention to take
over the International Settlements at Shanghai
and Kulangsu, and (d) that in distinction to
the hostile feeling in Japan against Great
Britain there was a general demand for improve-
ment in relations with the U.S., and that he
hoped before long that something concrete would
be accomplished.
Mr.

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
Mr. Arita alluded also to Japan's need of
developing her economic security. Finally he
indicated thtt the Japanese Government was pre-
pared, if the United States should desire that
some step be taken by the Japanese Government
for the purpose of dispelling American appre-
hension of future Japanese aggression in the
Philippines, to enter into conversations with
the United States Government.
Two things appear strongly emphasized:
(1) Japan's belief that its security as a
nation depends upon obtaining a position of
political and economic dominance in the Far
East and the fear that Great Britain may be
able to combine with Soviet Russia to obstruct
current Japanese efforts to achieve such a
position, and (2) Japanese concern lest the
judgment and attitude of the American Govern-
ment toward the situation in China may create
conditions which could place Japan and the
United States on opposite sides in the event
of a general war originating in Europe.
Mr. Arita's statements indicate a desire
on the part of the Japanese Government to dis-
abuse the United States of any idea that Japan
is a totalitarian state and to conciliate this
country in so far as this can be accomplished
without compromising Japan's thesis with regard
to the "new order" in East Asia. This thesis,
however, is so entirely incompatible with the
American position that the possibility of recon-
ciling the two view points through conversations
between

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
between the two governments at the present
time would seem to be very remote. Should,
however, Japan eventually be forced by the
burdens of its present ambitious program in
China to modify its thesis, there might then
be a possibility that conversations between
the two governments, especially if such con-
versations were at the initiative of the
Japanese Government, would be productive of
results.

9221

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
June 16, 1939.
Tokyo's despatch no. 3905 of May 20,
1939, entitled "The National Spiritual
Mobilization Movement", refers to the
organization early this year of the National
Spiritual Mobilization Committee to act as a
liaison organ between the non-political
Central League for National Spiritual
Mobilization and the Government and states
that the first plan of importance to be
adopted by the Committee was approved by a
Ministerial Conference on April ll. The
plan of April 1l is vague and incoherent,
although it contains frank recognition of
the evils caused by the growth of "war-time"
industries and the determination to correct
these evils.
The Embassy reports that on April 28
the Cabinet approved a plan described as
"the foundation" of the National Spiritual
Mobilization movement. This is summarized
on pages 5 and 6 and calls for the destruc-
tion of the Kuomintang and the ousting of all
third power interference in China. The plan
contains a number of exhortations of what the
Japanese people must do to assist in bringing
Japan successfully through the present crisis.
The

=== Page 185 === (confidence 0.99)
