Sir:
I have the honor to refer to !r. Grew's despatch
no. 3778, March 28, 1939, in regard to national mobili-
zation and the evolution in Japan toward certain aspects
m
bf socialism, and to report that the movement for national
spiritual mobilization alluded to in brief in the despatch
under reference has recently been crystallized into more
concrete forms than it has hitherto assumed.
It will be recalled that the press and public dis-
played considerable dissatisfaction with the activities
of the so-called Central League for National Spiritual
Mobilization
F/FG

=== Page 187 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Mobilization since its creation in October, 1937, cri-
ticism centering around the fact that the League was
without official status, and that as a result of this
criticism, it was decided by the Cabinet on February 9,
l939, to create a new organ to be entitled the National
Spiritual Mobilization Committee, the purpose of which
would be to act as a liaison organ between the non-poli-
tical Central League for National Spiritual Mobilization
and the Government. Since the formation of the new organ
in February a number of committee meetings have been held
and several plans for the translation into action of the
ideal known as "national spiritual mobilization" have been
adopted by the comittee.
The first plan of importance to be adopted by the
National Spiritual Mobilization Conmittee was approved
by a inisterial Conference on Anril 1l, 1939. An out-
line of the plan as given in the SHUHo (Weekly Cabinet
News) of April 19, 1939, is given below:
object:-
Plans for the establishment of the new order
in East Asia are being rapidly brought to com-
pletion. However, developments in Europe as well
as in Asia are difficult to forecast. Accordingly,
the Japanese people should be prepared to face any
emergency which may arise. Since the outbreak of
the China incident all classes of the population
have faithfully cooperated with the Government
in its endeavors to formulate and carry out what
has been virtually a war-time policy. It is now
deemed imperetive that the movement for national
spiritual mobilization be carried still further
in order to achieve the object of Japan's military
campaign in China, i.e., the establishment of a new
order in East Asia.
The

=== Page 188 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
The following factors are regarded as essential in
the acceleration of the movement for national spiritual
mobilization:
1. The creation of unity between the Government
and the people.
2. The thorough-going cooperation of the various
Government organs.
3. The full cooperation of various public bodies
with the Central League for National Spiritual Mobi-
lization.
4. Realization on the part of government, intellectual,
and business leaders of their obligations to the state.
5. The education of the rising generation and women
in the household in the necessity of complete co-
operation.
6. The recognition of practice and training in
daily life as a first principle, the control of mass
movements, and the exclusion of any tendencies toward
formalism in these movements.
7. The exercise in cities and towns (in the light
of past experience) of moceration and restraint in
all things, in particular the exercise of such re-
straint on the part of those engaged in "inflated"
industries.
3. The determination of measures to meet parti-
cular conditicns in certain areas, and the concen-
tration of all energies upon these measures with a
view to attaining the objectives of the movement
within the next year, that is, the 26ooth year of
the founding of the Empire.
The vague, incoherent nature of the foregoing plan
will be readily a parent. Much of its contents are little
more than exhortations couched in language so esoteric that
the junior officials for whom it is intended, if not con-
fused by it, are free to give it an endless variety of
interpretations. This much is clear: the spiritual mobi-
lization of the Japanese people for the tasks which lie
ahead

=== Page 189 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
ahead of them in East Asia is still regarded as insuffi-
cient; the present as well as the oncoming generation
must be educated to the ideal of a new order in East
Asia and this education must begin in the home; there
is a frank recognition of the evils caused by the growth
of "war-ti e" industries and a deter ination to correot
these evils; finally, the measures to be taken must give
due regard to local conditions.
Alarming inflationary tendencies at home and a
realization on the part of the Government that its military
campaign in China, not to speak of its plans for the
economic exploitation of the conquered areas, is still
no nearer achievement have pparently driven the Govern-
ment to a further clarification of the aims of the movement
for national spiritual mobilization, for on April 28 the
Cabinet approved a plan which has been described variously
as the "foundation" of the national spiritual mobilization
movement . In the lengthy preamble to the plan, the text
of which was published in the SHUHO for May 3, 1939, it
is pointed out that Japan is facing a crisis. Whether
the nation is to continue its epoch-making strides in
the history of the world depends on the outcome of the
present incident. Accordingly, the people at large must
be made to realize these facts and must resolve to
accomplish Japan's "divine mission" no matter what
difficulties may arise. The people must be made to
understand clearly:
first,

=== Page 190 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
first, that the settlement of the present incident
demands the complete destruction of the Kuomintang,
whose precepts contradict the national constitution
of Japan, and the establishment of a new order in
East Asia, tasks which Japan alone is fitted to
undertake. Second, that the first step in the
stabilization of East Asia, an essential factor
for world peace and international righteousness,
is to rescue East Asia from its colony-like condi-
tion. Third, that the anti-Comintern pact must be
strengthened and a joint front against Communism
effected in East Asia. Fourth, that Japanese culture
must be propagated throughout East Asia, thereby
purifying itself and giving new life to Chinese
culture. Fifth, that the construction of a new
order in East Asia depends upon the formation of an
East Asietic economic bloc. Sixth, that the do-
struction of the Kuomintang and the construction
of a new order in rast Asia require the ousting
of all third-power interferece in China; and that
in the confused international situation Japan as the
only great power in East Asia must establish itself
independently as the leader of East Asia. Seventh,
that the eccom lishment of Japan's divine mission in
East Asia requires the replenishment of armaments,
the expansion of productive capacity, the control
of co modity prices, and the adjustment of the supply
and demand of connodities.
The aims of the new plan for national spiritual mobi-
lization are to be achieved through the following methods:
1. Livin of simple lives -- every phase of public
and private life must be reformed and living mad
simple and suitable for the task of long-term con-
struction lying ahead.
2. Economical use of comnodities -- in view of th•
present situation with respect to demand end supply
it is essential that a movement be begun for the
economic use of commodities; that the habit of using
commodities completely and for every conceivable
purpose be encouraged; and that waste be prevented.
3. Utilization of vacant and waste lands -- a
movement must be started for the utilization of vacant
and waste lands through the employment of labor
battalions in reforestation and other projects.
4。

=== Page 191 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
4. General economy of consumption -- in the light
of the existing situation drastic restriotions in
the consumption of war materials, imported commo-
dities, and commodities which can be used for export
must be encouraged.
5. The use of non-essential or little used articles --
the use of non-essential or little used articles is to
be encouraged through dealers' organizations or other
bodies set up for that purpose.
6. Restoration of waste materials -- the re-
storation of waste materials is to be encouraged
through the organization of waste dealers under
Government supervision or other bodies set up for
that purpose.
7. Collection of gold -- the movement for en-
couraging the people to sell gold to the Government
is to be strengthened and movements for the abolition
of the use of gold ornaments are to be organized
in each locality.
8. Savings -- the movement for the encouragement
of savings must be strengthened in order to attain a
goal of Yen 10,000,000,000.
While there is little new in the prefatory statement
of the purposes behind the movement for national spiritual
mobilization, we do have a rairly clear definition of what
the Government expects of the people and an interesting
and authoritative restatement of Japan's "musts". There
is no evidence that the nation has receded in any way from
the position taken on November 3, 1938, when Prince Konoe,
then Premier, related Japan's determination to create a
new order in East Asia, a determination which would entail
the destruction of the Kuomintang regime. Likewise, there
is no indication that the economic stresses and strains
which the nation has undergone since that time have altered
the determination of the Government to see the China incident
through, albeit the tremendous difficulties involved are
fully recognized. Moreover, there is stated here in
unmistakable

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
unmistakable language the conviction that the estab-
lishment of the new order in East Asia requires the
rescue of China from foreign (non-Japanese) vassalage
and complete elimination therefrom of third-power in-
fluence.
It will be noted that a large element of exhortation
persists in the recitation of the methods by which spiritual
mobilization is to be achieved, exhortation aimed in part
apparently at the luxurious living of classes engaged in
the so-called "inflated" industries, a development which
has given the Government no little concern in recent
months. Nevertheless, the methods by which the goal of
national spiritual mobilization is to be attained do
include, for the first time, some fairly concrete measures
of compulsion: local organizations are to be formed under
the aegis of the Government for the conservation of materials,
the utilization of waste lands, the use of substitute materials
and the restoration of waste materials. The Government has
not yet been compelled, however, to order the confisoation
of private holdings of gold or put into effeot compulsory
sevings plans :or capital levies, still relying, so far as
these matters go, on the time-honored method of exhortation.
It is true that the demands for reconstruction of the state
which have been heard in increasing frequence in recent
months and which have formed the background for a not in-
considerable amount of political unrest are gradually
being met through the invocation of various clauses of
tho

=== Page 193 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
the National General Mobilization Law. Whether this plan
for National Spiritual obilization conceived and formulated
by the Hiranuma Cabinet will serve to allay such political
unrest as remains is still a moot question. The question
might well be asked why all these measures are necessary
in view of the many economic and financial controls which
have already been imposed upon the nation. The answer
probably lies in the character of the Japanese peoplo; they,
like the Germans, have a profound respect for authority,
but that respect has its weak side. They must be told
clearly and definitely what they can and ca not do. Lack of
positive prohibition amounts to license of action. The new
plan for spiritual mobilization ray be regarded more or less
us a res onse to this need: it provides the necessary
directives for the Japanese people during the struggle
which they are facing.
How successful these measures will be is another
question. Viewed as a part of the general movement toward
socialism in Japan, as they inevitably must be, their
success will undoubtedly hinge upon that of the numerous
economic and financial controls already imposed. General
economy of consumption and the use of waste and substitute
materials probably stand in a fair way of accomplishment.
Whether any tremendous increase in savings will take place
is problematical: certainly the official exhortations to
live simply and to forego amusements will be more effective
in this direction than hitherto, accompanied as they un-
doubtedly will be by a certain amount of intimidation on
the

=== Page 194 === (confidence 0.93)
- 9 -
the part of the local officials on whom the obligation to
see them fulfilled ultimately rests. Through the execution
of this program of national spiritual mobilization and
various related measures Japan will have placed another
notch in its belt, a belt which would still appear to
have a number of notches left.
Respectfully yours,
Lusque  Doonmay

68
19
S

eased to the press, the details apparently being
purposely shrouded in the deepest secrecy. In fact it is
understood on good authority that the Cabinet Ministers
were

=== Page 198 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
were cautioned not to reveal under any circumstances
the nature of the decisions taken on the occasion of the
adoption of the plan. The press itself seems to have
discreetly and studiously avoided any commentary since
the publication of the outline.
According to the outline which appeared in the
vernacular press the principal features of the Commodity
Mobilization Plan as announced y the Cabinet Planning
Board on May 27, 1939, are as follows:
1. Expansion of Productive Power
A prerequisite for the development of
a nation's strength is the increase of
rrocuction, an end which can only be
accomplished by an expansion of productive
power. Indeed, this is a factor of great
importance in considering both present and
future mobilization plans. Accordingly,
a proportionately large allotment of materials
has been made with this end in view, the
allotment being restricted, however, to an
indispensable minimum required to accomplish
the maximum result. Likewise, steps are to
be taken to mobilize the capital and labor
necessary for the expansion of production.
To supplement the shortage of materials, special
measures will be taken to gather together
ordinary as well as waste commodities.
2.

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
2. Promotion of Foreign Trade and Increase
of Tmports.
Under existing circumstances the supply
of domestic products is not sufficient to
meet the demand for materials arising from
the China incident. In order to pay for the
large amount of foreign materials required,
it is essential that Japan's export trade
be maintained and developed. In the execution
of the present plan priority is to be given
to raw materials used in the manufacture of
oods for export, and capital and labor are
to be mobilized specifically for the production
of s cl. oods. The domestic consumption of
co modities produced with imported raw materials,
as well as the domestic consumption of purely
domestic oods which are capable of being
exported, is to be restricted so far as possible.
In order to increase Japan's purchasing power
abroad the pro uction of gold is to be encouraged,
measures will be taken to collect privately-held
gold, and the traveling of Japanese abroad will
be restricted.
3. Aid to Manchukuo and China.
In order to maintain peace and order in
Manchukuo and China and to develop their
resources, which are essential elements in the
settlement of the China incident and the
establishment of a new order in East Asia
and

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
and hardly less important in the expansion
of productivity in Japan proper, various
materials are needed. Accordingly, endeavor
has been made in the compilation of the present
plan to provide materials for Manchukuo and
China to the reatest possible extent.
4. Econony in Daily Necessities.
Considerable economy must be effected
in the consumption of daily necessities,
allowance beins made, however, for items
essential to the national health and food
su ply, such as drugs and fertilizers.
5. Distribution of Laterials.
The main task of the conmodity mobilization
plan is to distribute commodities into the
appropriate channels. Commodities have, therefore
been divided into six categories: munitions,
government needs, materials for the expansion
exports
of production,/yen-bloč needs, and domestic
needs. In order to ensure proper distribution
it may be necessary to institute ration and
coupon systems for certain commodities.
6. Regulation of Consumption.
A much more rigid economy in the consumption
of commodities must be brought about this year
than was effected last. Not only must there
be

=== Page 201 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
be thorough retrenchment in the consumption
of daily necessities but also in the
consumption of commodities required for
industries of major importance. While
it is hoped that this aim may be achieved
through voluntary response of the people
in ceneral it may be necessary to institute
certain control measures.
7. Iobilization of Communications and
Electric Power.
In the mobilization of communications
and electric power, the backbone of national
defense and industry in general, measures are
to be taien in te sea, land, air communica-
tions, trans ortation, and electric power
industries to ensure maximum efficiency,
particularly in relation to the production
of munitions, the expansion of production
generally, the promotion of foreign trade,
and the adjustment of demand and supply of
materials and labor.
The plan, according to Mr. Kazuo Aoki, head of the
Cabinet Planning Board, was formulated with the basic idea
in mind of settling the China incident and establishing a
new order in East Asia, but the immediate objectives of
the plan are the replenishment of munition stocks, the
perfection of national defense, the promotion of foreign
trade, and the development of "Manchukuo" and China.
Despite

=== Page 202 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
Despite the fact that no details are available,
and from all inuications will not be made public for
military reasons, the foregoing outline serves as a useful
guide in studying the metamorphosis which is taking place
in Japan's economic life. Early this year it became
increasingly clear that the more important provisions of
the National General Mobilization Law would be invoked
in rapid succession. By May a number of these had been
invoked. In the same month an elaborate plan for the
"spiritual mobilization" of the nation was made public by
the government. It was noted at that time that a large
element of exhortation persisted in the methods by which
spiritual mobilization was to be achieved but that some
fairly concrete meusures or compulsion had been introduced
for the first time. In a sense the plan for spiritual
mobilization set the stage and prepared the people at large
for the present plan for commodity mobilization. Through
the adoption of this plan Japan's economy may be said to
have entered another stage. Compulsion becomes dominant,
exhortation only secondary, in most phases of Japan's economic
life.
From statements made by the Cabinet Planning Board from
time to time, it appears that the principal articles in
which increased production is sought are: steel, coal, light
metals (aluminum, magnesium, etc.), non-ferrous metals,
petroleum oil and substitutes, industrial soda, ammonia
sulphide, pulp, gold, industrial machinery, rolling stock,
ships, automobiles and trucks, wool, and electric power.
Acoordingly

=== Page 203 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
Accordingly, raw materials as well as capital and labor
will be diverted to these industries. The production
of certain comodities such as sulphuric acid, nitric acid,
and glycerine will develop, it is believed, without
any particular plan. The food industries have been left
out because Japan is regarded as self-sufficient in this
respect. Likewise, such commodities as rubber, cotton,
and caustic soda lhave been excluded from the plan on the
grounds that Japan is unable to reach any degree of self-
sufficiency in them in a short period of time. However,
the question of assuring their supply is to be considered
separately.
With respect to the expansion of Japan's export trade,
reater emphasis is to be laid on the importation of those
raw materi ls viich can be used in the manufacture of
export goods. Capital and labor will be impressed by the
government into these industries, and consumption of goods
which may be used for export will be drastically restricted.
Gold production is to be encouraged by means of the
liberalized provisions of the Japan Gold Production Encourage-
ment Company law. With respect to the collection of
privately-held gold another gold census is to be taken
on July 1, l939, which would seem to indicate the continuation
of voluntary methods. However, it is not improbable that
measures of compulsion to obtain privately-held gold will
be instituted at an early date. There have been recent
indications that travel of private Japanese individuals
abroad, already restricted, will be almost completely eliminated,
thus

=== Page 204 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
thus effecting savings in foreign exchange.
With respect to the question of the yen-bloo in
the commodity mobilization plan there seems likely the
extension of a policy which has been followed for some
time. Buildings have been left nfinished in Japan beoause
of lack of steel, while new projects are carried forward
in Hsinking an Mukden. In North China the Japanese
military have consistently followed the policy of integrat-
ing.the economy of that area to Japan proper. That policy
will probably be continued with greater emphasis on
providing materials for North China to rehabilitate
industries there.
It will be noted that the distribution of commodities
is to be made according to six categories: munitions first
and domestic needs last. It is considered likely that
certain commodities will have to be rationed and the coupon
system instituted. Gasoline, it will be recalled, has been
under the ration system since March 1938. It is important
to bear in mind that such systems if instituted will be
only for certain commodities. They will undoubtedly be
unnecessary for foodstuffs, parallels now being drawn by
many foreign observers between conditions in Soviet Russia
and Germany and Japan being greatly misleading in this respect.
On the other hand, certain daily necessities, now bought and
traded in freely, will undoubtedly undergo a certain amount
of restriction. With respect to the communications industries,
transportation, and electric power industries, the operations
of these industries will be geared more closely to the
demands of munitions, foreign trade, and the yen-bloc.
Meanwhile

=== Page 205 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
Meanwhile the other parts of Japan's socialized
economy are gradually being fitted into shape. It was
reported on June 8 that the Cabinet Planning Bureau had
completed plans for the invocation of Article 4 of the
National General Mobilization Law which provides for the
recruitin of specialized types of labor for certain purposes
by the overnment. The reorganization of the Ministry of
Commerce and Industry with its price bureau for the
control of prices, plans for which were reported in
Mr. Grew's despatch no. 38l4 of April 8, 1939, appears
certain of adoption in June. Coupled with these various
changes the cry for adninistrative reform which has been
more or less smothered since the fall of the Konoye Cabinet
early in 1939 is arain heard. Whether it will be given
ear by the overnment is problematical. The system for
the reform of the civil service which has been hanging fire
for almost a year has finally emerged in greatly emasculated
form. Nevertheless, events are proceeding so rapidly that
certain minor administrative changes will probably be
inevitable. In any case as the conmodity mobilization plan for
this year goes into effect Japan seems to have entered a
new stage in its economy and its history.
Respectfully yours,
Eugone H. Dooman

July 21, 1939.
Tokyo's despatch no. 3783 reports on
progress of the movement in Japan, under the
basic National Mobilization Law (i938), toward
economic self sufficiency. Provisions of the
Law, which were sharply debated in the Japanese
Diet and applied but slowly upon becoming law,
have this year been "rapidly called into play."
Acceleration of the movement is attributed to
the "political and military situation on the
Continent" (China) and "inflationary tendencies
at home."
Efforts toward economic self sufficiency
(styled "auterchy" by the Embassy) have taken
the concrete form of a "mobilization plan"
adopted in May 1939 (Embassy's despatch no.
3955, June 9, 1939), under which it was sought,
in the interest of export trade, to decrease
"(1) domestic consumption of commodities pro-
duced with imported raw materials and (2) do-
mestic consumption of purely domestic goods
which could be used for export." The former
objective is reported near achievement and the
latter receiving the full force of official
encouragement. Concurrently, the movement
toward self-sufficiency is "receiving active
encouragement in almost every industry in
Japan, noteworthyitems in which self-sufficiency
is sought being steel, coal, light metals, non-
ferrous metals, petroleum oil and substitutes,
industrial soda, ammonia sulphide, and pulp.
Restricted consumption, in connection with
efforts to become self-sufficient, has
:depressed

=== Page 207 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
depressed living standards which, however, are
"still vastly higher than . . twenty years
ago", and the Embassy points out that the
Japanese are peculiarly fitted to clothe and
feed themselves independently of other nations,
and that there appears little likelihood of
the Japanese people rebelling against still
further restrictions - political or economic.
On the contrary, the Embassy notes that a pro-
found spiritual movement or "rallying to the
cause of the Emperor" is taking place, defying
intellectual analysis but nevertheless facili-
tating "transition from laissez-faire capital-
ism to autarchy."

in regard to the Hational Mobilization Movement and to
report upon certain phases of this movement which are now
becoming clear for the first time.
The basic legislation for the present movement, the
National General Hobilization Law, has now been inctorge

future purposes. By the end of 1938 developments in

the nore irportant provisions of the National Genoral

Counled with these epoch-making measures and their
attendant effect on the political and economic life of
Japan is the eneral movement toward autarchy or economio
self-sufficiercy, a development, the evolution of which
has been somevhat obscured by the sound and fury raised
by the imposition of the measures of regimentation. As
reported in my despatch no. 3955, June 9, 1939, the
adoption of a so-called commodity mobilization plan for
the year 1939 presa ed a new stage for Japan's economy.
In that plan, it vill be recalled, considerable stress
was laid on the development of Japan's export trade. In
the accomplislment of that objective there was expressed
the necessity thet domestic consumption of cormodities
produced with imported raw materials and domestic consump-

export be greatly decreased. The former objeotive would

now appears to be in the process of receiving the full

domestic consumption of butter, cheese, and other food-
stuffs, many of which have become an important adjunot,
although not in any sense staple, to the Japanese iet,
seems likely at this writing to be prohibited entirely
in order that these commodities may be used solely for
export. Already many domestically produced items of
wearing apparel of distinctively foreign style but normally
used by the Japanese people have disappeared from the shops,
being forcibly directed, it appears, into the channels of
foreign trade. Simultaneously vith these developments the
Cabinet recently announced the adoption of a plan for a
Foreign Trade Council, which is in effect a compromise
over the demands of the Arny for a Foreign Trade Ministry,
such as is co mon to Japan's totalitarian friends in
Europe, and the purely advisory body now in existence.
It is understood, however, that the Army received assurances
from the Prime Minister that the newly-organized Foreign
Trade Council under the jurisdiction of the Ministry of
Commerce and Industry would not remain an ordinary liaison
organ but would engage actively in the promotion of foreign
trade and would eventually become the nucleus for a full-
fledged Trade inistry.
These developments are not without their obverse side.
Along with the encouragement of foreign trade, the res-
triction of domestic consumption of foreign goods, and the
domestic

=== Page 211 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
domestic consumption of domestic goods whioh might b
1
used for export, has been the move toward autarohy,
which is receiving active encouragement in almost every
industry in Japan today. This movement began in a very
small way with the compulsory use of staple fiber in'
order to obviate the necessity of paying for raw materials
for cotton and woolen goods. It has now grown by leaps
and bounds to a degree that no stone is being left unturned
in the endeavor to discover substitute products in ordor
that Japan may become completely self-sufficient for all
ordinary purposes. As mentioned in my latter despatch under
reference the items in which Japan seeks self-sufficiency
are: steel, coal, light metals (aluminum, magnesium), non-
ferrous metals, petroleun oil and substitutes, industrial
soda, amonia sulphide, pulp, in fact almost every commodity
which is regarded as essential in Japan's present-day
economic organization. hile concrete measures toward
the production of substitutes are not given publicity, it
is known that many of the principal industries in Japan
are already spending considerable time and money solely
on the experimental production of substitutes for articles
which ordinarily must be imported from abroad. Among these
might be mentioned the production of cloth fabrios from
glass and rock-wool, the encouragement of shale oil production
and the development of various substitutes for gasoline.
With these developments Japan's living standards,
already somewhat on the decline, can hardly avoid further
depression. It must be borne in mind, however, that these
standards

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
standards are still vastly higher than those prevailing
twenty years ago. For such a future the Japanese pooplo
would seem to be well-prepared. For conturies, due to
geographical reasons and otherwise, they have had an
extremely insular, feudal outlook. Moreover, their
contact with the western world has been of short duration.
They are able, for example, to clothe and feed themselves
independently of other countries far more satisfactorily
than Germany and Italy whose cultural and material contaots
with the rest of Europe are far more deep-seated and
compelling in charccter.
With the invocation of the various provisions of
the Tational General Mobilization Act the bureauoratic
controls necessary for Japan's excursion into autarchy are
already in place. Canital and labor may be ordered into
certain fields of production and kept out of others. Price
control machinery has already been set up. Little remains
to be done other than await the inexorable effect of economic
pressure arising out of Japan's adventures on the Asiatic
mainland. With respect to the general question of the
attitude of the Japanese people toward autarchy and all that
it entails it may be pointed out here that although only a
year has passed since a serious dispute on the question of the
imposition of the ational General :obilization Law ocourred
in the Diet, the Japanese people have already accepted that
legislation as an integral part of their life and from all
indications there vould appear to be little likelihood of
their rebelling against still further restrictions on their
politioal

=== Page 213 === (confidence 0.97)
-6-
political and economic activity. At the same time, ther
are evidences that the feudal relationship between employor
and employee, a characteristic of Japanese industry even in
modern times, is being deepened and extended. Moreoever,
a profound spiritual movement appears to be taking place in
Japanese life akin to that experienced during the Restoration,
a KINORON or rallying to the cause of the Emperor, which
defies all attempts at intellectual analysis but which
nevertheless exists in strength probably far greater than
any appeals to otan or heerkening back to the glories of
ancient Rome. An inevitable result of that movement can only
be a smoothening of the path of transition from laissez-faire
capitalism to autarcliy, a path which appears to have been
forced upon Japan by the march of events. The Embassy will
endeavor to provide a more detailed analysis of this movement
at the earliest opportunity.
Respectfully yours,

more wages than ever before, and higher than
normal spending is conspicuous among such
workers. Cn the other hand, the salaried

preveiling conditions,but they are the least

The group least convinced of the wisdom of the

government more and more in business, business
men are more and more powerless to impose their
viewe.

controls over consumption has been surprisingly
smooth. The public in general 1s not aware
of divided counsels in the lesderehip of Japan,
although among the highest advisers of the

lack of background possessed by the men who
have risen to the army's leadership.

MR. HORNBECK
AUG 241939
Washington
Sir:
Estimates of Japanese fighting strength generally
take into account the morale of the people, and to
assist in appraisal of the value of such estimates it
SEP141939
therefore appears worth while to attempt a study of the
FILHD
state of the public mind of Japan upon the conclusion
F/FG
of two years since the commencement on July 7, 1937, of
the current hostilities in China.
With

=== Page 223 === (confidence 0.99)
1256
-2-
With regard to the course of the military campaign
in China the Japanese public thinks with a certain degree
of candor. That a quick and complete viotory was not
attained is realized; that the campaign is in course of
spreading endlessly is realized; the cost in lives and
goods is more or less realized. The early peak of flag-
waving enthusiasm is long past; but there is, on the
other hand, a nearly unanimous conviction that the
hostilities and the sacrifices are necessary. They are
accepted and faced in much the attitude that the nation
from time immemorial has assumed when confronted by
natural disaster. The entire nation is focusing upon
the conduct of the hostilities much the same reaction
which any locality of the country would bring to the
facing of conditions following an earthquake.
As to public content or discontent over economic
matters, the key to the state of mind lies in the war
boom which is now in full swing. Boom conditions are
natural during a period of extensive hostilities, and
present-day Japan is no exception. In a large range of
essential industries skilled labor is receiving more
than ever before, and higher-than-normal spending is
conspicuous among such workers. Controlled sale of con-
sumption goods is a factor limiting to the channels of
outlet open to this spending, and more of it goes into
services, less into commodities, than would be the
normal tendency. The amusement places and vacation
resorts are prospering and new ones have opened; travel
and

=== Page 224 === (confidence 0.99)
1257
-3-
and excursion trips are at a new high; the theaters
are crowded. Labor has little to complain of. It is
rather the salaried people and the business men who,
in the shadow of rising prices and of increasingly
stringent control of enterprise, are hit by the pre-
vailing conditions. On the other hand, they are the
very classes which, in these times, can least afford
to be articulate in opposition. Any opposition which
is articulate must be prepared to face the political
philosophy of those now in the saddle, which is dominantly
leveling, no matter what its terminology. Circumstances
therefore conspire to keep at a minimum opposition
arising from economic causes.
By and large, the group least convinced of the
wisdom of the China policy is the business men.
Their
experience accustoms them to the conviction that always
after large expenditures there is a day of reckoning;
they have been hard hit by the limitations of controlled
economics; they are less convinced than are the political
philosophers among the military that projects in China
are sound business. But what is perfectly obvious is
that, with the Government more and more in economics,
business men are more and more powerless to impose their
views.
The state of mind reflected by the continuing ab-
sorption of public loans for the hostilities is not a
significant indicator. Such continued absorption is a
consequence of controlled finance in this country and
is not useful as a criterion of confidence.
The

=== Page 225 === (confidence 1.00)
1258
-4-
The carrying into effect of the various controls
over consumption has been surprisingly smooth. Gasoline,
tires, cotton goods, and construction and repair work
are the categories most seriously affected thus far.
While the restrictions must inevitably give rise in the
Japanese people to concern, the state of mind aroused
is in no sense panicky.
Our knowledge of divided counsels in the leadership
of Japan should not lead us to the error of magnifying
the divergences of view into a cleavage in the public
mind. It is true that the young and radical military
group (as has very recently been made plain in connection
with the Tientsin question) exerts a strong and a dangerous
power over the Japanese Government; but it is not true
that the people are aware of this conflict nor are
divided by it. It is cause for great concern among the
highest advisers of the Emperor that the officering of
the army has, in accordance with the army's principle of
recruiting from the common people, come to lie with indi-
viduals who lack the responsible restraints which come
with the background of family and tradition. Under a
system in which, constitutionally, the military enjoys
extraordinary exemptions, there is genuine concern about
the lack of background of the men who have risen to the
army's leadership. But the people have not been taken
into confidence in this, and the public state of mind is
unaffected.
Toward the Chinese, Japanese feeling has shown
notable restraint. Chiang Kai-shek and his party are
vilified

=== Page 226 === (confidence 1.00)
1259
-5-
vilified, but otherwise sentiment is held in check.
So marked is this that there is in it confirmation for
the thesis that Japan's conflict in China is aimed not
at the Chinese but at the definitive ending of occi-
dental control of, and influence over, China.
Toward Great Britain feeling is bitter, nor is
this confined to the leaders. So intensive has been
the campaign in the press against Great Britain that a
sudden reversal would now be impossible--nation-wide
anti-British sentiment is already a fact. The antagonism
is already emotionally a serious factor in any dealings
which there may be between Great Britain and Japan. The
lack of restraint in allowing (or directing) the Japanese
press at an early stage in the current hostilities to
unleash a continuous attack on Great Britain suggests
that from the inception of the conflict there was a
deliberate acceptance of the inevitability of an eventual
Japan-British impasse over Great Britain's imperial
possessions in Asia. The resentment that has been nursed
for many years against Great Britain for having terminated
the Anglo-Japanese Alliance has, of course, predisposed
the Japanese mind to seek an opportunity to settle accounts
with their former ally; and the present situation in
Europe presents an opening which is not being overlooked.
Toward Germany the public mind looks with enthusiasm
rather than with friendliness. Germany is not admitted
as a partner in an ideal new order of things in Asia. In
that new order Japan would dominate unchallenged. Meantime,
however

=== Page 227 === (confidence 1.00)
1260
-6-
however, mutual self-interest is served by acting together
on the international stage. Aggressions are thereby
mutually reinforced, and Japan can feel herself less
completely an international outcast.
Toward the United States the sentiment is, among
the people, one of admiration and fundamental friendli-
ness. Among persons in authority it is all too plain
that every effort is being made to keep our interests
from coinciding with British interests, and this is
patently and indisputably a deliberate design dictated
by self-interest. r. Yukio Ozaki, famous parliamentarian,
has asserted as much.
Either present observation or a
memory that goes back not even far is sufficient ground
for the conviction that the Japanese Government has ready
to hand the means of creating, at any moment dictated by
policy, a Japanese public opinion directed against the
United States. However, Jepanese leadership was, we
believe, profoundly impressed by the President's message
of April 14 to Hitler, was startled into a realization
of the dangers of international complications for Japan
if tied to German interests in European antagonisms, and
was moved to refuse closer ties with Germany for the
dominant reason of desiring to avoid a clash of interest
with the United States.
Any estimate of the state of mind of Japan must
take account of the handicaps of the average Japanese
in the ordinary processes of thinking. Students of
propaganda do not have great respect for any nationality
in

=== Page 228 === (confidence 0.99)
1261
-7-
in the mass, as regards powers of resistance to manu-
factured ideas; but in the instance of the Japanese
there is even greater ground for pessimism. The Japanese
are particularly vulnerable to confusion in thought by
reason of their susceptibility to abstract words. The
point can be illustrated by any Japanese newspaper,
where article after article will be found to be ab-
stractly constructed out of common generalities, relieved
by a minimum of event. This susceptibility has made the
official jargon concerning the hostilities peculiarly
effective. "A new order in eastern Asia", "the spirit
of Japan", "the foundations of a permanent peace", are
phrases which inspire and convince the people, whereas
facts like the death of hundreds of thousands of Chinese
on Chinese soil escape attention. Nor is this the first
point in Japanese history when abstractions have exerted
an influence over the people. Their literature and
their history are full of illustrations. The indigenous
view of suicide might be cited; also the academic con-
flicts which led up to t1 eiji restoration. Now, as
no doubt in the past too, some individual Japanese think
more bluntly. Some today occasionally state that Japan
desires to control China, can do so, and will. The vul-
nerability of the general population to the abstract
formulas simply makes the leadership of the few that
much easier.
The colors in which the Japanese Government has
painted the China conflict for internal consumption by
the Japanese people have in the main been successful,
from

=== Page 229 === (confidence 0.99)
1262
2
-8-
from the Government's point of view, in depicting the
conflict in the manner desired by the Government. But
however internally successful has been Japanese propa-
ganda, it would be erroneous to suppose it entirely
effective. The particular respect in which it has
failed is in the effort to prevent among the people a
sense of isolation on the world stage. In this, Japanese
propaganda has not accomplished its object. The cen-
sorship has not succeeded in quarantining the people
against adversely critical views expressed abroad, and
there has permeated through the population a heavy
oppressive consciousness of censure and of isolation.
Friendly relations with Germany and Italy have been ex-
ploited in considerable part for the very purpose of
dispelling the sense of being an outcast in isolation
among the nations of the world. International censure
has made itself known in one form or another to the
Japanese people, and although a considerable risk is
thereby run in the accumulation of hatred, possibly ex-
plosive at some future time, it appears that, in balance,
the United States (given its existing Far East policy)
gains rather than loses through keeping alive, by occa-
sional published statements and by notes protesting
Japanese actions in China, Japanese realization of American
disapproval of their policy in China. There has been
consistency in American disapproval, and while it has
provoked strong dislike it has at the same time given riss
to an attitude towards us which comes very close to respect.
Bespectfully yoprs,
Eugene H. Dooman

Tokyo's despatch no. 4035, July 12,
1939, reports the creat1on on July 4, 1939,
of the Asia Promotlon Commission, a quas1-
official organ for dealing with matters
under the Jurlsdiction of the Asia Promotion
Board. The new Commission will apparently
largely duplicate the Board in its work and
membership, and 1s in effect a board to watch
a board. Its immediate origin lies in the
conflict of ideas between the Cabinet and
the bureaucracy. The press and public are
apathetic as to the effectiveness of the new
institution. When requested by the Director
of the Board, who 1s also Chairman of the
Commission, the Commission is to inquire into
and deliberate upon important matters relating
to the work of the Board. The Commission
1s the supreme advisory organ to the
Director of the Board in formulating Japan's
China policy, and as such 1s important since
responsibility will be diffused. The main
conclusion to be drawn from this and
similar developments in present-day Japanese
political life is that the Japanese people
are by no means as of one mind as to ways
and means for carrying out aims in China.

I have the honor to report that under date of July 4,
1959, there was pro ulgated, and published in the KANPO
(Official Gazette) on July 5, 1939, an Imperial Ordinance
providing for an Asia Promotion Commission, a quasi-official
the Asia Promotion Board. There is enclosed a translation
by the Embassy of the ordinance in question.
The

=== Page 233 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The new Commission is to be composed of not more than
fifty members under the supervision of the Prime Minister.
Whenever requested by the Director of the Asia Promotion
Board, who also acts as Chairman of the Commission, the
Commission is to inquire into, and deliberate upon, im-
portant matters relating to the work of the Asia Promotion
Board. With regard to these matters the Commission is
empowered to make recommendations to the Director of the
Asia Promotion Board. The ordinance provides that the
members are to be appointed by the Cabinet, upon the recom-
mendation of the Prime Minister, from higher government
cfficials and men af learning and experience. Routine work
of the Commission will be delegated to the staff of the
Asia Promotion Board.
Appointments to the new Commission, which came into
being simultaneously with the promulgation of the enacting
ordinance, have already been made by the Prime Minister, and
they include, along with an executive committee composed for
the most part of officials of the Asia Promotion Board, Vice
Ministers from the different government departments, and
Cabinet Councillors, many of the more important figures in
Japanese professional and industrial life, among whom might
be mentioned Mr. Yosuke Matsuoka, former head of the South
Manchuria Railway Company, Mr. Shozo Murata. head of the
Osaka Shosen Kaisha, Dr. Hantaro Nagaoka, President of the
Imperial Acadeny, and Mr. Shingo Tsuda, head of the Kane-
gafuchi Spinning Company. The relationship between the
newly-created Commission and the Asia Promotion Board is
expeoted

=== Page 234 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
expected to resemble that between the Cabinet and the
Cabinet Advisory system. As the supreme advisory organ to
the Director of the Asia Pronotion Board in formulating
Japan's China policy, the new Commission would appear to
be cestined to occupy an extremely important position
in the chaning fabric of Japan's administrative structure.
The creation of the new Commission is characteristic
in many ways of the working of the Japanese political mind.
The Asie Prouction Boerd alreedy hes renresentatives of the
different cvernment departments in its composition, and
overnment representatives sit on each of the lieison com-
mittees betveen the Boarc and the various government depart-
ments. Likewise the  oard hes drawn heavily on industry and
tie professicns for aid in formulating policy. Yet a new
orean is created whici duplicates in many respects what has
already beer set up. Had the composition or the Commission
been so changed as to reduce the preponderance of official
and nilitary elenents, one might find defensive argument
for it from the field of Occidertal political theory. As
it is, we can only conclude that the Commission is a product
of the confusion of Japanese political thought. It is, in effect,
a board to watch a board. Its immediate ori in lies, of course,
in the conflict of ideas between the Cabine, which is in it-
self a potpourri of political thought, and the bureaucracy.
Press and public are apathetic as to the effectiveness of
the new institution. One of the prircipal criticisms heard
is that the very fact that the commission is composed of so
many

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
many different elements pr:cludes its genuine effective-
ness as a policy-makine boay. At the most, it is felt,
the Commission will lend dignity and authority to the actions
of the Asia Promotion Board. A further consideration which
was no doubt in the mind of the Government in setting up the
Commission is that the latter vill tend to diffuse responsi-
bility. The main conclusi n which may be drawn from this and
similar developments in present-day Japanese political life is
that the Japanese people are by no means as of one mind, as
many wetern observers believe, as to ways and means for
carrying out aims and objectives in China. A conmon error and one
vhich would appear to be of fundamental importance is the popu-
lar Occidental assunption that the Japanese are so methodical
that they have each and every step of a progran worked out long
in advance. This half-truth often leads to profound misjudg-
ments and is likely to have far-reaching consequ ences in matters
of policy.
Activities of the newly-created Asia Promotion Commission
will be reported promptly to the Department as they occur.
Respectfully yours,
Lagpur

I have the honor to refer to previous despatches
from this Embassy in regard to national mobilization
in Japan and to report that there was recently pro-
ASSISTANT SECRETARY
mulgated an Ordinance for the National Draft, which
provides for the compulsory drafting of certain types
OF STATE
of labor for national mobilization purposes. A trans-
山
lation of the present ordinance which will be effective
from July 15, 1939, in Japan Proper and from October 1,
0
1939, in Chosen, Taiwan, Karafuto, and the South Sea
1/
Islands, is enclosed with this despatch. A translation
of ministerial regulations, providing detailed rules for
the enforcement of the draft in Japan Proper from July 15,
2/
1939, is also enclosed.

=== Page 241 === (confidence 0.93)
- 2 -
The present ordinance is based on Article 4 of
the National General Mobilization Act which reads as
follows:
"In time of war when it is necessary to
national general mobilization, the Government
may in accordance with the provisions of an
Imperial ordinance draft Imperial subjects or
may cause them to engage in general mobilization
enterprises. Application of the Conscription Law,
however, shall not be interfered with."
The background for the present ordinance was already
laid down in January, 1939, with the enforcement of an
ordinance (reported in Mr. Grew's despatch no. 3640 of
January 30, 1939), for the compulsory registration of
the vocational abilities of skilled workers in a wide
category of industries inter alia as follows:
Skilled laborers in the machine tool industry.
1n
n
n
electric and telegraph
industries.
n
n
n
n
glass industry.
9"
n
9"
canning industry.
1"
n
mining industry.
Coal miners.
Builders of inside frames of coal and other mine
galleries.
Lens inspectors.
Machine repair mechanics.
Workers who assemble machine parts.
n
n
parts in the aircraft, auto-
mobile, and shipbuilding industries.
Wood workers.
Draughtsmen.
Crane operators.
Telegraph (cable) operators and laborers.
Gold workers (gilding).
Painting.
The Ordinance for the National Draft provides that
persons required to register under the vocational
abilities ordinance may be drafted for national mobili-
zation work under the authority and at the direction of
the Minister of Welfare. Remuneration is to be made in
accordance

=== Page 242 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
accordance with the drafted person's technical ability,
previous experience, and other related factors. Travel
expenses to and from the place of employment are to be
provided by the state. The following classes of persons
are exempted from the operations of the national draft:
1. Soldiers or sailors in active service (excluding
persons not yet enlisted) and persons under call
(including persons receiving the same treatment
as persons under call).
2.
Students of military or naval schools (including
naval reserve students and naval reserve con-
tinuation students).
3.
Civilians in military or naval service (excluding
persons already drafted who later come to fall
under this category).
4.
Persons concerned with medical treatment required
to register under the Ordinance for the Regis-
tration of Vocational Abilities.
5.
Veterinary surgeons required to register under
the Ordinance for the Registration of Vocational
Abilities.
6.
Crew members within the meaning of the Crew Law,
crew members under the Korean Ordinance for Crew
Members, and crew members under the ordinance of
Kwantung Territory for Crew Members.
7. Persons under detention pursuant to laws or
ordinances.
Except in the case of special necessity the following
three classes of persons are also exempted:
1. Government officials, persons treated as govern-
ment officials, and public officials, not in
position to be replaced by others.
2.
Members of the Imperial Diet, prefectural assem-
blies, municipal assemblies, town councils, vil-
lage assemblies, and similar assemblies.
3.
Persons engaged in general mobilization work,
who cannot be replaced by others.
The

=== Page 243 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
The original purpose of Article 4 of the National
General Mobilization Law was to provide the Government
with the authority to draft nationals in national mobili-
zation work required in the ordinary course of the pro-
secution of war. The scope and character of the so-called
China Incident have been so greatly extended and altered,
however, that this article as well as others of the
National General Mobilization Law have come to assume
when enacted into ordinance greater and what would appear
to be much more lasting significance. The basic pro-
visions of Article 4 in its narrower sense had already
existed in much earlier legislation, namely the Munition
Industry Mobilization Law of 19l8 which was abrogated
when the National General obilization Law came into
force on April 1, 1939. Because of its wide implica-
tions Article 4 was the subject of particularly acri-
monious debate during the 1937 session of the Imperial
Diet which saw the passage of the National General Mobili-
zation Law. Not even then, however, had the possibilities
of the measure if earnestly employed penetrated the con-
sciousness of the Japanese people at large. As a con-
comittant to the China Incident there has occurred in
Japan a rapid and enormous increase in the demand for
labor in war time industries and in industries designed
to make Japan self-sufficient economically. This need
has been greatly accentuated by the conscription of large
numbers of skilled workers for active service in the
Army in China. In the face of this demand ordinary
mothods

=== Page 244 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
methods available to a capitalistic state such as the
setting up of special employment agencies, special
inducements to skilled workers, the creation of voca-
tional training agencies and the like have been un-
availing, with the net result that many industries in
which the employment of skilled workers is essential
are facing grave deficiencies in personnel. The present
ordinance is a frank recognition that the present state
of Japan's economy requires the compulsory drafting of
labor. In the framing of the present ordinance the
Cabinet Planning Board (KIKAKUIN) has admitted that
German and British models of labor conscription legis-
lation, particularly the former, have been consciously
imitated, and indeed there is very close resemblance.
A question which immediately comes to mind, of
course, is the relative permanence of this and similar
socialistic measures which have been imposed upon the
Japanese people since the outbreak of the China Incident.
It would seem to us that the tasks with which Japan is
faced on the continent are so great that an almost com-
plete subordination of Japan's economic and political
structure to the prosecution of these tasks is inevitable.
Accordingly, the trend towards socialism in Japan will,
it appears likely, expand and assume features of per-
manency in direct proportion to the efforts required to
build what is vaguely termed a new order in East Asia.
The imposition of the present ordinance for labor
conscription

=== Page 245 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
conscription and related measures is tantamount to a
bloodless revolution, the outcome of which it would
be extremely difficult to predict. Suffice it to say
the die seems to have already been cast and the trend
is moving in a direction from which, saving any develop-
ment short of defeat in war or internal strife, it would
appear impossible to be deflected.
Respectfully yours,
Eugene H. Dooman
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim

Imperial Ordinance No. 451
Ordinance for the National Draft.
Article l. Except as otherwise provided, the drafting
of Imperial subjects by virtue of Article 4 of the
National General Mobilization Law shall be in accor-
dance with the provisions of this ordinance.
Article 2. Drafting shall be carried out only when the
personnel needed is not obtainable through the medium
of employment agencies and other methods of recruit-
ment, except in thos instances where there are special
reasons.
Article 3. Drafting shall be limited to those persons
who are required to register by virtue of the
Ordinance

=== Page 247 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Ordinance for the Registration of National Voca-
tional Abilities hereinafter referred to as "persons
required to register", provided that this rule shall
not apply to those instances where persons in the
course of the draft no longer "persons required to
register", are still needed for the draft.
Article 4. Persons drafted under this ordinance shall
be made to engage in national general mobilization
work carried out by the Empire.
Article 5. Draft and discharge from draft shall be
enforced in accordance with orders of the Minister
of Welfare.
Article 6. When the competent Minister of the govern-
mental office (including military and naval detach-
ments and schools, hereinafter the same) carrying out
national general mobilization work deems it necessary
to assign personnel to the governmental office con-
cerned, he shall apply therefor to the Minister of
Welfare.
Article 7. In the case of an application made under the
provisions of the preceding artiole when the Minister
of Welfare deems such drafting necessary, he shall
issue a draft order and notify the prefectural gover-
nor having jurisdiction over the dwelling place of the
person to be drafted (the place of operation in the
case of a person engaging in occupations mentioned in
no. 1, Article 2 of the Ordinance for the Registra-
tion of National Vocational Abilities).

necessary from the standpoint of the protection of
military secrets, the whole or a part of the matter

dwelling place (place of operation in the

of the Ordinance for the Registration of

3

occupation and place;
4.

Translation by the American Embassy at Tokyo of Ordinance
No. 17 of the Ministry of Welfare promulgated on July 1l,
1939 and published in the Kanpo (Official Gazette) of the

A. F.
Ordinance No. 17 of the Ministry of Welfare.
The regulations relating to the enforcement
of the Ordinance for the National Draft have
been determined as follows:
July 11, 1939 (14th year of Showa)
Hisatada Hirose
Minister of Welfare
Regulations relating to the enforcement of
the Ordinance for the National Draft.
Article l. An order for the draft, order for the al-
teration of the draft or order for cancellation of
the draft issued by the Minister of Welfare shall
be conveyed in writing. However, in the case of
urgency which renders writing impossible, such
orders shall be by telegraph (urgent official
telegram).
Article 2. When deemed necessary to determine the
suitability of the draft and other matters, the
prefectural governor may cause the heads of employ-
ment agencies to request persons who are to be
drafted to report in person.
Article

=== Page 258 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Article 3. When the prefectural governor or the head
of an employment agency requests persons who are to
be drafted, to report in person, he shall issue a
summons in accordance with form no. l hereto and
serve the same upon the latter.
Article 4. When a person who is to be drafted receives
a summons, he must appear in person with the summons
at the specified place and at the specified time and
report to the official concerned.
Article 5. A written order for the draft, a written order
for amendment of appearance, a written order for can-
cellation of the draft, a written order for alteration
of the draft, and a written order for discharge of the
draft shall be drawn up in accordance with form no. 2
appended hereto.
Article 6. A written order for the draft, a written order
for amendment of appearance or a written order for the
cancellation of the draft shall be served upon the
person who is to be drafted or who is drafted, through
the heads of employment agencies or mayors, of towns,
villages, (ward chiefs in the cities of Tokyo, Kyoto,
Osaka, Nagoya, Yokohama and Kobe) or other persons
corresponding thereto.
Article 7. When a person who is to be drafted or who is
drafted receives a written order for amendment of
appearance or a written order for the cancellation of
the draft, he shall enter the date of receipt upon
the receipt attached to such an order and forthwith
return it after affixing his seal thereto.
Article

=== Page 259 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Article 8. When a person who is to be drafted or who
is drafted receives a written order for the draft
or a written order for amendment of appearance, he
shall appear in person with such order at the specified
place at the specified time and report to the official
concerned.
Article 9. The report to be made under the provisions
of paragraph 1, Article ll of the Ordinance for the
National Draft (hereinafter referred to simply as the
Ordinance) shall be accompanied by the following docu-
ments and be made to the prefectural governor issuing
the written order for the draft.
1. In the event it is impossible to appear in person
owing to sickness or injury, a medical certificate
of a physician (in the case of unavoidable circum-
stances which render it irpossible to obtain a
medical certificate of a physician, a certificate
of a police official).
2.
In the case of an act of God or other unavoidable
reason which renders it impossible to appear in
person, a certificate of a mayor of a city, town,
or village, or other person corresponding thereto,
police official, ship captain or station master.
Article l0. A written order for the amendment of the
draft or written order for discharge of the draft
issued by the Minister of Welfare shall be conveyed
to the prefectural governor issuing the written order
for the draft prior to the appearance of a person
drafted at the specified place, to the prefectural
governor having the jurisdiction over the place of
appearance

=== Page 260 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
appearance in case a person drafted has already
appeared in person at the specified place, and to
the prefectural governor having the jurisdiction
over the place of operation in the case person
drafted is engaged in general mobilization work.
Article ll. A written order for amendment of the draft
or written order for discharge of the draft issued
by the Minister of Welfare under the provisions of
paragraph 2 of Article 16 of the Ordinance shall be
conveyed through the chief of the governmental
office employing a person drafted in case such a
person is engaged in general mobilization work.
Article l2. The provisions of the preceding article
shall apply mutatis mutandis to those instances
where a written order for discharge of the draft is
served by the Minister of Welfare under the pre-
visions of paragraph 3 of Article 16 of the Ordinance.
Art. le 13. When a person drafted receives a vritten
order for amendment of the draft or written order
for discharge of the draft, he shall enter the date
of receipt on the receipt attached to such order and
forthwith return it after affixing his seal thereon.
Article 14. When deemed necessary, the Minister of Wel-
fare and the prefectural governor may require reports
in regard to the draft from a person to be drafted
or from persons who are employing or have employed
the person to be drafted.
The report mentioned in the preceding paragraph
may be required by the heads of employment agencies
in

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
in the case of urgent need or with respect to minor
matters.
Article 15. When the competent official makes an
inspection under the provisions of paragraph 2,
Article 20 of the Ordinance, he shall carry a card
made according to form no. 3 appended hereto.
Supplementary Provision
These regulations shall be enforced from
July 15, 1939.

A telegram (no. 450) of September 1, 1939, from the
American Embassy at Tokyo reads substantially as follows:
Mr. Williams (American Commercial Attaché) has pro-
pared a study concerning the effeots which war in Burope
would probably have on the trade and industry of Japan.
A
In this study it is pointed out that there should be a
noticeable inorease in the demand for American goods --
especially wood pulp, cotton, iron, steel, non-forrous
metals, chemioals inoluding fertilizers and dyesturfs,
maohinery and other industrial equipment -- as an immed-
iate effect of war in Europe. The study reveals, however,
that the United States as a source of supply to Japan would
be very much inoreased as a result of an interruption in
the flow of the above mentioned commodities from countries
in Europe and of primary materials from British areas over-
seas. Japan's present productive capacity would be les-
sened seriously and her program of industrial expansion
would break down completely without increased amounts of
American supplies of iron, steel, oil, machinery, cotton,
chemioals and pulp. The Chargé is of the opinion that this
is a correot conolusion, and that therefore a desire for
renewal of good relations with the demooratic powers would
develop in Japan as a result of economic expedieney eom-
bined with considerations of a political charaster.

The despatch states that General Abe's
Cabinet is not important for its composition
but for the circumstances which brought it
about, the manner in which it was formed, and
the shape which it hes assumed; that the
most compelling reason for Baron Hiranume's
resignation was his desire to assume personal
responsibility for the failure of his Govern-
ment to foresee the conclusion of the
German-Soviet non-aggression pact, and the
second and lesser reason was to manifest out-
wardly the beginning of a new "independent"
policy; that Hiranuma apparently felt that
by his resignation he would not only clearly
establish the responsibility of the Prime
Minister and redefine its position vis-à-vis
the Throne, but would bring into clear relief
the role which to his mind the Throne should
play in Japanese politics; that Hiranuma
will probably become one of the principal
"behind-the-scenes" statesmen; thet the
selection of Hiranuma's successor was largely

Mr. Yuasa,

No. 4126
SUBJECT:
CHANGE IN THE JAPANESE CABINET - THE RESIGNATION
OF BARON HIRANUMA AND THE APPOINTMENT OF GENERAL
ABE AS PRILE MINISTER.
844.0
