ASSIBT NT SECRETARY
Sir:
OF STATE
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's telegrams
F/WB
Nos. 435, August 25, noon; 437, August 25, 7 p.m.; 440,
A0mp.m.; 447, August 30, 9 a.m.; and subsequent telegrams
in regard to the fall of the Hiranuma Cabinet and the forma-
tion of a new Cabinet under General Nobuyuki Abe.
As

=== Page 290 === (confidence 0.99)
1281
-2-
As set forth in my first three telegrams under refer-
ence, the reasons behind the resignation of the Hiranuma
Cabinet were to manifest outwardly the end of the policy
of cooperation with Germany and Italy under the anti-
Comintern pact and the beginning of a new "independent"
policy, and the desire of the Prime Minister, Baron Hira-
numa, to assume personal responsibility for the failure of
his Government to foresee the conclusion of the German-
Soviet non-aggression pact. We are now able to analyze
the effect of the conclusion of this pact upon the Japa-
nese mind with sufficient perspective to conclude that the
last reason given was perhaps more compelling than the
first two. For months Prime Minister Hiranuma, with the
aid principally of his Navy Minister, Admiral Mitsumasa
Yonai, had been fightin off the Army's demands for closer
relations with the Rone-Berlin axis, and that fight had
been largely successful. In the normal workings of Japa-
nese politics it would have been possible, despite the
changed external situation, for the Prime Minister to
jettison the War Minister, General Itagaki (indeed, the
Army offered to assume complete responsibility for the
new development), and carry on as before. That Baron
Hiranuma did not follow this course is undoubtedly due
to the character of the man himself. His statement made
to the press on August 29 explaining the reasons for his
decision to resign is of sufficient importance to warrant
quoting in full:
"Ever since I assumed office I have constantly
endeavored, in full cooperation with my colleagues
and

=== Page 291 === (confidence 0.99)
1282
-3-
and in accordance with the Imperial will, to surmount
all obstacles and to effect the construction of a new
order in East Asia in order that the objects of the
holy war in China might be attained. In foreign re-
lations a primary requisite has been to follow a
policy of contributing toward world peace and civil-
ization on the basis of the spirit of the national
foundation and moral principles. With this object
in mind I gave consideration to Japan's European
policy and reported thereon to the Throne from time
to time. However, the conclusion of the German-
Soviet non-aggression pact gave rise to a strange
and complicated situation in Europe. In view of this
situation it became necessary for Japan to abandon
the policy which had been prepared and to establish
a different one. This clearly represented a change
in whut I had been frequently reporting, thereby
causing inconvenience to the Throne once more. I am
overawed by the great responsibility to the Throne
confronting me, and I fear that my remaining in office
any longer would involve, in view of my duty as sub-
ject to Emperor, disrespect to the Throne. Moreover,
I believe that in order to overcome the emergency
facing Japan through internal reorganization and re-
orientation of Japan's foreign policy, the most urgent
problem of the day is to alter the face of affairs
and to revitalize the minds of the people. For the
foregoing reasons I have today tendered my resignation
to the Throne."
It seems clear from Prime Minister Hiranuma's statement
that his resignation was motivated primarily by the doctrine
that

=== Page 292 === (confidence 0.99)
1283
-4-
that the first duty of the Prime Minister as subject is
to respect and enhance the dignity of the Throne. To con-
tinue in office under the changed situation would, in
Baron Hiranuma's mind, require exercise of imperial clem-
ency, thus detracting from his prestige as Prime Minister.
But what is more important, he felt apparently that by
his resignation he would not only clearly establish the
responsibility of the Prime Minister in matters of this
kind and redefine its position vis-à-vis the Throne, but
would bring into clear relief the role which to his mind
the Throne should play in Japenese politics. The above
analysis would appear to be borne out by the fact that the
press was not only unanimous in its praise of Prime Min-
ister Hiranuma's decision to resign but for several days
after the event gave only secondary importance to the
selection of the new Premier, concentrating most of its
attention on the character of Baron Hiranuma and the reasons
behind his resignation. Out of the present development
Baron Hiranuma seems destined to emerge as one of Japan's
outstanding "behind-the-scenes" statesmen, his power and
influence growing in inverse proportion to the actual
responsibilities which he has now divested from himself.
As secondary considerations in the decision of the
Prime Minister to resign, it would appear that he and his
advisers felt that the situation demanded a clearing of the
air and that only through withdrawal would the new govern-
ment be left completely free to initiate such new policies
as the changed situation might require. The thought must
have been strong in the mind of the outgoing Premier that
the heritage he had assumed from the Konoe Cabinet in re-
gard to the settlement of the China incident had often
risen

=== Page 293 === (confidence 0.99)
1284
-5-
risen to plague him and that the Cabinet to come, if it
were to surmount the tremendous difficulties facing Japan,
must be free from similar legacies.
An outline of the mechanics by which General Nobuyuki
Abe was selected to form a Cabinet to succeed that of Baron
Hiranuma was provided in my telegrams Nos. 445, August 28,
10 p.m., and 446, August 29, 3 p.m. The decision of re-
turning Japan to a new "independent" policy free from
European commitments had already been announced by the
Hiranuma Cabinet through its chief secretary on August 25.
11
The problem then arose of selecting someone to carry out
this policy. This task appears to have fallen largely on
cil and Minister vithout Portfolio in the Hiranuma Cabinet;
Mr. Kurahei Yuasa, Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal; and
Count Nobuaki Makino, former Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal,
OB
all men with a sound understanding of Japan's international
position and its present internal political situation, and
all possessed of intangible political power. It appears
that these leaders, when it became clear that Hiranuma would
not continue in office, decided upon three men who they
thought were most fit for the Premiership: Mr. Koki Hirota,
Foreign Minister in the Saito and Okada Cabinets and Prime
11
Minister in 1937, General Kazushige Ugaki, Foreign Minister
for a short time in the Konoe Cabinet, and General Nobuyuki
Abe, retired. Apparently Mr. Hirota was soon eliminated
as a candidate because of his close identification in many
minds with Japan's foreign policy in the past, in particular
possibly his attitude of conciliation towards the Soviet
Union. In the latter respect he could not, of course, ex-
pect the support of the Army. : The name of General Ugaki
was brought forward and was given long and careful consid-
eration,

=== Page 294 === (confidence 0.99)
1285
-6-
eration, but was finally rejected largely because of the
unfortunate circumstances attending his resignation in
1938 when, as Foreign Linister, he opposed the establish-
ment of the Asia Promotion Board, and also because of his
abortive attempt to form a Cabinet in l937, both memories
still fresh in the minds of the people. The choice then
fell upon General Abe, a man completely unknown in civil
politics and without particular distinction in the Army,
although he had served for a short period as Acting War
Minister in the Hamaguchi Cabinet during the illness of
General Ugaki.
One important fact stands out in bold relief in the
present Cabinet change: despite the Army's dominant posi-
tion in the st te and the fact that the power of direction
over the affairs of state lies, for all practical purposes,
in its hands, a substantial residuum of power over import-
ant matters of state, a power of decision as contrasted to
power of direction, still is lodged elsewhere, in the group
around the Throne, of which the Lord Keeper of the Privy
Seal, Mr. Kurahe: Yuasa, is the focal point. This is not
to assert that there exists a cleavage of power within the
Japanese state itself: civilian elements see eye-to-eye
with the Army on matters of principle. Such differences
as may arise are essentially rather those of method to be
pursued. That the group around the Throne could make its
influence so effective in the present Cabinet change was
probably due to the logic of the situation itself: the
Army, discredited by the implications for Japan of the
German-Soviet non-aggression pact, was prevented for the
moment from taking active part in the political maneuvers
required

=== Page 295 === (confidence 0.97)
1286
-7-
required for the Cabinet change, such influence as the Army
could bring to bear being of a negative, reflected character.
The Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal Mr. Yuasa, Prince Konoe,
and others were able, therefore, to proceed relatively
free-handed in the establishment of a government which
would bring Japan's foreign policy back on the track of
4
Japan for itself and freedom from European commitments.!
一
It is of interest to note that Prince Saionji, the sole
一
surviving Genro, was completely ignored by Mr. Yuasa, the
Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal, in selecting a new Premier,
being consulted only after the selection had taken place,
一
thereby indicating that the Genro may be discounted in the
future as an effective force in Japanese politics.
As reported in my telegram No. 447, August 30, 9 a.m.,
the composition of the Abe Cabinet is as follows:
Prime Minister: General Nobuyuki Abe, concurrently
Minister for Foreign Affairs.
Minister of War: General Shunroku Hata.
Minister of Navy: Vice Admiral Zengo Yoshida.
Minister of Home Affairs: Mr. Naoshi Obara,
concurrently Minister of Social Welfare.
Minister of Finance: Mr. Kazuo Aoki, concurrently
President of the Cabinet Planning Board.
Minister of Commerce and Industry: Mr. Takuo
Godo, concurrently Minister of Agriculture.
Minister of Communications: Mr. Ryutaro Nagai,
concurrently Minister of Railways.
Minister of Education: Mr. Kakichi Kawarada.
Minister of Overseas Affairs: Mr. Tsuneo Kanemitsu.
Y
Chief Secretary of the Cabinet: Mr. Ryusaku Endo.
The

=== Page 296 === (confidence 0.99)
1287
-8-
The new Premier, General Abe, has been most aptly
described as an unknown quantity. His name was not even
mentioned until a few days before he received the command
from the Throne and his selection came as a complete sur-
prise to the Japanese press and public alike. As previously
mentioned, his sole political experience in civil adminis-
tration consisted of a few months as Acting War Minister
in the Hamaguchi Cabinet during the illness of General
Ugaki. He is not without political reputation in the Army,
however, being known as a man of caution but of exceptional
courage (he is best remembered for his part in the Army
retrenchment program of 1925), thoroughly honest and pos-
sessed of considerable perspicacity. He has made few enemies,
and what is more important, he is not known to favor any
fixed set of policies, both of which factors appear to have
been paramount in his selection and which constitute at the
same time both his strength and his weakness. Son of a
samurai family, General Abe represents the old and fast-
disappearing type of gentry-soldier in the Japanese Army.
Like many of the older group in the Army, General Abe saw
service abroad as Military Attaché in Germany and in Austria,
and is, therefore, presumably familiar with western ways of
life and thought.
Abe's Cabinet, with the exception of Admiral Yoshida,
his Navy Minister, a man with a brilliant naval record and
one long slated for his present post, and perhaps Mr. Kawa-
1
rada, a close friend and confidant of Prince Konoe, is
一
without distinction and may be dismissed with a few words.
1
The Minister for War, General Shunroku Hata, will be remem-
bered as the Commander-in-Chief of the Japanese forces in
Central

=== Page 297 === (confidence 0.99)
1288
-9-
Central China who replaced General Matsui shortly after
the sack of Nanking. He is a quiet, rugged type from whom
nothin6 spectacular is expected. The non-service members
1
1
of the Cabinet are for the most part professional bureau-
crats like Mr. Aoki, the Finance Minister, and with a de-
4
cided "controlled economy" background, or professional
office seekers like Mir. Ryutaro Nagai, the new Communica-
tions and Railway Minister. It may be mentioned parenthet-
ically that while General Abe paid lip service to the prin-
ciple of including Party members in his Cabinet, he ignored
the custom
of waiting for overtures but proceeded to pick
the men he desired and informed the Parties after the event.
General Abe's Cabinet is not important for its compo-
sition but for the circumstances which brought it about,
the manner in which it was fomed, nd the shape which it
hes assumed. By the systen of concurrent portfolios the
total number of Cabinet members has been brought down to
ten. The latter principle was adopted, it appears, for
three reasons: first, to avoid the danger of an imperium
in imperio which manifested itself in the workings of the
Five-Minister Conference principle under the Konoe and
Hiranuma Cabinets; second, to facilitate despatch of Cabinet
business; and third, to smooth, through coordination of
activities, the path of internal reform which is now taking
place in the various Ministries.
From all indications the Abe Cabinet will follow
closely the policies of its predecessors in domestic matters,
no marked changes being anticipated for the time being.
The

=== Page 298 === (confidence 0.99)
1289
-10-
The considerations arising in regard to foreign affairs in
respect of the Abe Cabinet have been discussed in a separate
despatch.
Respectfully yours,
Eugene H. Dooman

JWB:
Tokyo's despatoh no. 4139 of September 20,
1939 reports the five planks of the Abe
cabinet's platform, the gist of which is that
the "China incident" must be settled, a new
China Government established, and that the
national general mobilization movement must
go on. The Embassy reports that the platform
was not enthusiastically received by the
press and that some papers attach significance
to the fact that the Prime Minister did not
use the phrase "establishment of a new order
in East Àsia" but instead used "new relations
between Japan and China".

=== Page 316 === (confidence 0.95)
THE FOREIGN SERVICE
OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
AMERICAN EMBASSY
Tokyo, September 20, 1939
No. 4139
SUEJECT:
PLATFOR ANNOU.CED BY THE CABINET OF PREMTER ABE.
88
4
AFTER
REK
1939 061
894.00/878
The Honorable
130
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
1/
I have the honor to enclose herewith a copy of a trane-
lation of the platform adopted by the present Cabinet as
reported in the Japan Advertiser of September 14, 1939.
This platform is contained in five principal points as fol-
A
lows:
1. Concentration

=== Page 317 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
1. Concentration of all efforts in domestic
and international affeirs toward a settlement of
the China incident and, as a result, development
of the solidarity between Japan and "Manchukuo" and
of new relations between Japan and China.
2. Positive assistance to the establishment
of a new regime in China (presumably that of Wang
Ching-wei).
3. Expansion of the means of production under
a definite, comprehensive program involving Japan,
"Iianchukuo" and China, and the adjustment of foreign
trade.
4. Further adjustment and strengthening of the
national general mobilization structure, including
price control and mobilization of labor.
5. Institution or appropriate administrative
reforms in overnment agencies to assure proper
expedition of official business.
In publicly announcin a platform, the Abe Cabinet
has revived a custom which had been abandoned by the two
precedin : governments of Prince Konoe and Baron Hiranuma.
This platform couched in very general terms is not of
itself particularly illuminating. It does, however, re-
iterate with some emphasis the determination to "settle
the China incident", and in this connection to give
positive assistance to the establishment of a new Govern-
ment in China. The repeated statements of government
officials and of the press that the settlement of the
China incident is to be pushed vigorously and that a regime
wil1

=== Page 318 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
will soon be established indicate that Japan expects some
success in the near future in the attempts to establish a
new Government in China which will be sufficiently repre-
sentative to enable Japan to recognize that regime and to
come to terms with it. In this connection the recent
establishment of one command, under General Toshizo Nishio,
of all the expeditionary forces in China is to be noted.
Points three, four and rive have to do with measures,
largely domestic, looking to further regimentation and
control of Japan's economy.
This has been called a na-
tional seneral mobilization movement.
There have been
recently indications that a Forein Trade Ministry is
under consideration. The administrative machinery has not
in many instances proved capable of expediting sufficiently
the various complicated adjustments, controls, permits and
investigations necessary to the system of controlled economy
which has developed in Japan.
There have, accordingly,
been many voices raised seeking administrative reforms
and the Government has promised to accelerate its efforts
in this direction.
The reception given this platform by the vernacular
press was not enthusiastic and many papers did not hesitate
to point out that the people were more anxious to see con-
crete results than to read of broad general plans. Some
papers attached significance to the fact that the Premier
did not use the phrase "establishment of a new order in
East Asia" but instead used "new relations between Japan
and China". The press was unanimous in its approval of
an independent diplomatic policy and of the emphasis placed
2/
upon a settlement of the China incident. A copy of the
Japan

=== Page 319 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
Japan Advertiser's translation of several of the vernacular
papers' comments on this subject is enclosed.
It seems apparent the present Government in Japan
will urgently seek a solution of the China incident through
the establishment of & new Government in China, will attempt
to devise measures to adjust Japan's foreign trade in the
li ht of new and developing world conditions, such as the
closing or possible closiny of former markets and sources
of supplies including the United States, and will, at the
same time, continue und expand the program of national
regimentation and rationalization of the nation's economy,
atteaptin un expansion of productive capacity and re-
plenishment o: materials. These represent practically no
new objectives, but s I have pointed out the emhasis on
the settlement of the China incident, the establishment
of a new Government in China and the apnrehension with
which Japan's future roreign trade is viewed are significant.
Respectfully yours,
Tisme T Doruey
Eugene H. Dooman

The first reaction of the press to the "insubor-

favorable to their case. Undoubtedly the unity and fixed
purpose of the Foreign Office officials, which were
closely exhibited throughout the negotiations which
followed, impressed the Japanese editors to some extent,
and articles placing at least a portion of the blame for

=== Page 331 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
Foreign Office are adamant in their insistence that
commercial attachés and representatives of a similar
nature be under the administrative control of the
Minister for Foreign Affairs.
In a brief conversation with me the Minister for
Foreign Affairs expressed considerable interest in
the recent amalgamation under the Department of State
of the various foreign services of the United States.
After my explanation he expressed the hope that a
similar situation could be developed in Japan, but
he stated that it was first necessary for the Ministry
of Foreign Affairs to become a department of influence
and prestige in the Government. From this I concluded
the Minister believed that under present international
conditions it was not important whether the Ministry
of Foreign Affairs, wielding as it does little or no
influence, retain control of the Japanese trade repre-
sentatives abroad or whether such control passed to
other hands.
There has been some suggestion in the press that
this present dispute has as its background fundamental
differences over the question of the policy to be main-
tained in the international relations of Japan. One
paper went so far as to suggest that the younger offi-
cials in the Foreign Office were insisting that a
strong stand be taken toward England and the United
States and that the Cabinet was attempting through
the setting up of this new ministry to bring about a
readjustment

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

December 30, 1939.
Consul Gourley in his report of Octo-
ber 18, 1339, entitled "Japanese Youth
Volunteer Summer Workers on Continent of
Asia", has compiled useful information which
merits perusal. He shows that, although the
work of settling Japanese in Manchuria (and
also Mongolia and north China) has not ad-
vanced far, during the summer of 1939 several
thousand Japanese youths were sent to the
continent as volunteer workers for the summer
months only and that this may result in some
substantial progress in the plan of encourag-
ing settlement. The Consul believes that the
experiment was successful and will probably
be repeated in future summers.
The work done by the youth volunteers
on the continent is said to have included
farming assistance, road building, construc-
tion of schools and other buildings, erection
of telegraph and telephone poles, construction
of sewers, guarding of highways, and miscella-
neous work for the Japanese army. In Japan
proper there is a training school or camp
with & capacity of 10,000 youths, which gives
trainine for settler-farmers for Manchuria.
Appended to the report is a map graphi-
cally showing the location of Japanese
immigrant settlements in manchuria.

=== Page 336 === (confidence 0.99)
Volunteers for Summer Work Only.
In the summer of 1939 thousands of youths passed
through Kobe en route to "Manchukuo", North China and
Mongolia. They were being sent to those rogions by
the Japanese Government to serve as volunteer workers
during the summer months only.
Types of Volunteers.
The youths ranged in age from 14 to 19. They
came from widely scattered areas of Japan. The groups
which went to North China and Mongolia and some of
those who went to "Manchukuo" were students who expected
to continue their studies upon coming back to Japan.
Others, however, intended upon their return to do farm-
ing and various types of work.
Prospects of Repeatin Sunmer Volunteer Experiment.
The experiment seems to have been successful and
will probably be repeated in future summers. For many,
if not all, the journey to the mainland of Asia was
an adventure and was an interesting way of spending a
vacation in spite of the hard work. For all the youths
the trip was expected to be instructive and to arouse
interest in areas under Japanese domination. An appeal
was made to the patriotism of the youths and they were
made to feel that they were helping their country and
the army on the continent.
Arousing of Interest in "manehukuo"
Many of the youths who went to "Mamohulouow From
darming commuhities might, it appaers to

=== Page 337 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
hoped, thus get acquainted with the opportunities in.
"Manchukuo" and directly or indirectly help in plans
for Japanese emigration to build up that country
Upon their return their leaders were advertised as
saying that they found the climate of "Manchukuo"
pleasant and suitable to Japanese, that most of the
youths desired to remain in "Manchukuo" or to go there
again, and that every one came to the conclusion that
all young Japanese, except the eldest sons of families,
should settle on the continent.
Useful Nature of Nork in "Manchukuo".
The work which the youths did was useful. In
"Manchukruo" they engaged in farming and also helped
in road building, the construction of schools and
other buildins, the erection of telegraph and telephone
poles, and laying down sewers. From speeches of their
leaders it is interesting to note that some apparently
went to parts of "ianchukuo" where there is still
political unrest, because part of their work was to do
guard duty along roads.
Aid to Army in North China and Mongolia.
The student worker groups who went to North China
and Mongolia did work for the Japanese army in those
areas and were also given opportunities to do sight-
seeing. Different groups went to such, places as
Paoting, Kalgan, Taiyuan, Tsinan, Tientsin and Taingtao.
Number

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.98)
amber of 1939 Sumer Youth Volunteer Workers:
The total number of volunteer youth worker
spent the summer of 1939 on the continent of Ae1
amounted to at least 11,O00. The majority* came baok
by sea to Kobe. In addition, not only workers from
eastern Japan but also some from the Kobe area and
western Japan returned through the Japan Sea port of
Niigata far to the northeast.
Preliminary Training in Pioneers' Camp.
Before leaving for the continent at the end of
June and the first part of July many of the youths
had taken training at a Volunteer Pioneers' Training
School at Uchibara in Ibaraki Prefecture northeast of
Tokyo.
Japanese Youth Volunteer Settler Movement.
The Uchibara training school or camp is capable
of accomnodating 10,000 youths. It is used especially
to prepare youths of preconscription age to become
trained settler-farmers in "Manchukuo". The Japanese
youth volunteer settler movement dates back to the
autumn of 1934, when a Japanese Army officer picked a
small group of Japanese youths for special training
at Jaoho on the northeastern border of "Manchukuo"
facin Soviet territory. The experiment turned out to
be very satisfactory and the Japanese Government be-
came convinced that it would be a good national policy
to

to send young Japanese between 16 and 19 years of age

youths undergo further training in imnigrant life in
one of several training camps in "manchukuo". Sub-
sequently they join various subsidized or independent
immigrant settlements where their training, example
and guidance are expecteu to be of much benefit.
Need of More Volunteer Youth Settlers.
Large suns of money have been set aside by the
Governments of Japan and "Manchukuo" to encourage
youth settlers to go to "manchukuo" for training and
permanent settlement. Although about 17,500 youths
had gone by the end of 1938, the interest is not as
great as was hoped for.
The small amounts of money disbursed to send youth
workers in the summer may be well spent if they come
back favorably impressed with "Manchukuo" under summer
conditions and serve as propagandists of the opportun-
ities there.
It is not easy to stimulate emigration of youths
these days to "Manchukuo", which has a winter climate
that does not appeal to the Japanese. With the con-
tinuance of the "China incident" and the noed of young
men for the army and for factories making munitions

have gone up so high that youths of pre-conscription
age are little interested in going abroad and are being
attracted from farms and villages to work in factories
in Japan itself.
Summer Youth Volunteer Work for Immigrant Settlements
in "Manohukuo" near Soviet Territory.
Many of the volunteer youth workers who passed
through Kobe in the summer of 1939 went to work in the
newly created Province of Tungan.* This Province is in
northeastern "Manchukuo" and touches Soviet territory.
Because of its strategic position the Japanese Govern-
mont wishes to encourage settlers of pure Japanese race
to live there.** It was glad to have the summer
volunteer youth workers do their bit, however small,
to make preparations for more immigrants.
Subsidized Mass Japanese Farmer Emigration to "Manchukuo"
The Japanese Government particularly desires to
have Japanese farmers settle with their families in
"Manchukuo". Although at the end of 1937, almost 420,000
Japanese*** were living in "Manchukuo", very few were
engaged in farming.

There has been some emigration from Japan into

There has also been subsidized mass Japanese farmer
emigration to "Manchukuo". A 1932 plan called for the
settlement of 100,000 Japanese farming households within
ten years, each immigrant farmer to receive a subsidy
of 1,060 yen. Yet from the autumn of 1932 to the spring
of 1937 only 2,785 farming households** went. A 1936
plan anticipated the settling of 1,000,000 Japanese
farming households involving 5,000,000 farmors in twenty
years. Under this plan some of the settlers were to
receive direct Government aid and protection, whereas
others were to receive aid from private organizations
or were to rely on their own resources. Abnormal con-
ditions brought about by the spread of hostilities in
China interfered and emigration has not inoreased every
year as planned.***
In the subsidized self-proteotive Japanese farm
settlements the Government has aimed to provide the
immigrants with necessary common equipment and facilities.

Youth Volunteer Settlers in "Manehukue"
While the subsidized mass immigration movement was?
thus taking a strong hold on the Asiatic continent,
another movement of equal importance was undergoing a
similar experimentation on the Manchurian soil. This
is a movement to train young people of preconscription
age and to settle them as trained settler-farmers in
Manchuria.
This movement dates back to 1934. The plan was
first hatched in the brains of Lieutenant-Colonel Tohmiya
and was actually realized on September 16, 1934, when 14
young people were settled at Jaoho in north-eastern
Manchuria. The members, ranging from 15 to 21 years of
age, were all picked youths from among a group recommend-
ed by those who were interested in the plan and began to
undergo a systematic training there. They were jointed
by additional 16 young people in July, 1935, and others
every year since that today there are 84 young people
cultivating 40 hectares of land. They form a part of
the Yamato Village at Jaoho at the present time.
The Jaoho experiment was so satisfactory and stired
up such a wide interest in the project that it brought
about a far-reaching result. First of all, the Manchuria
Colonization Society took it up and began to sponsor it
on a larger scale. By the summer of 1937, almost 300
youths were enlisted in this movement. While this work
of enlistment and preparations was in progress in Japan,
the plan was receiving an almost universal acclaim in
"Manchukuo". Thus, in July, 1937, met at Hsinking a
special conference to consider the question of Japanese
youth immigration into Manchuria. It was attended by
the representatives of the Kwantung Army, the Tokyo
Department of Colonial affairs, related Departments of
"Manchukuo" Government, the Manchuria Colonial Develop-
ment Company, the South Manchuria Railway Company, the
Manchuria Colonization Society, and other interested
organizations. After a careful review of the youth
volunteer settlement movement and an extended delibera-
tion concerning its bearing upon the general immigration
movement, it adopted a plan of training 30,000 youths in
3 years and of establislhing training camps as a first
definite step in translating the idea into action. The
Manchuria Colonization Society was to carry on the
activities of enlisting and dispatching in Japan and
the Manehuria Colonial Development Company was to
establish training camps and to institute training in
Manchuria. It was under this plan that 293 young
volunteers who were already enlisted and who were under-
going training at Uchihara in Ibaragi Prefecture up to
that time were settled at the Nonkiang Training Camp
which was established by the Manchuria Colonial Develop-
ment Company at llaha, north of Tsitsihar, during August
and September, 1937. Before the end of the year, 26
additional

=== Page 346 === (confidence 0.99)
additional youths jointed the original party and launh
a new movement of Japanese youths toward Manohuria.
In the meanwhile, a movement to make the youth.
volunteer settlement a part of the national polioy was.
crystallizing in Japan. As a result, six representa-
tives of the Manohuria Colonization Society, the Farm-
ing Village Rehabilitation Society, and the Japan
Federation of Young Men's Associations memorialized
the Government on November 3, 1937, and proposed to
make it a national policy to send 50,000 youth volunteer
settlers into Manchuria during 1938. It estimated the
required expenditures at 12,500,000 Yen. The Government
took this important matter up at a oabinet meeting on
the following November 30 and adopted a national polioy
of sending 30,000 youth volunteer settlers during 1938
through the cooperation of the Manchuria Colonization
Society in Japan and the Manchuria Colonial Development
Company in Manchuria. For 1939 and thereafter, new plans
are to be drawn up later in the light of the 1938 experi-
ences.
This new program thus adopted for instituting youth
volunteer settlements in Manchuria proposes to enlist,
train, and settle in Manchuria young people between 16
and 19 years of age and to furnish the backbone as well
as the leadership of future Japanese settlements there.
The enlisted youths, at least the graduates of primary
schools, first undergo a preliminary training for two
months at a training camp in Japan and then proceed to
Manchuria where they undergo further training in immi-
grant life for three years in one of several training
camps there. After these trainings and experiences,
they join various subsidized or independent immigrant
settlements. In training camps, they are organized in
a military fashion like the boy scouts and lead a highly
disciplinary life. The training consists of studies,
including ordinary subjects such as history, geography,
and language and special subjects such as Manchurian
agriculture, Manchurian affairs, utilization of agri-
cultural products, dietetics, and physiology and hygiene,
ethical and civic training, military discipline, and
technical education such as agricultural architecture,
farm life, practical village engineering, and repair
works. They also acquire practical experiences in the
management of farming settlements, including the super-
vision of farms, farming implements, large scale kitchen,
and libraries. Needless to add, the entire expense of
this new enterprise is to be borne by the Government.
The training camp in Japan, capable of accommodating
10,O00 youths, was established at Uchihara in Ibaragi
Prefecture, while the training camps in Manchuria were
established at five places as follows: the Nonkiang
Training Camp at Ilaha near Nonkiang, north of Tsitsihar,
in Lungkiang Province; the Ningan Training Camp at
Shalanchen near Ningan, south of Mutankiang, in Mutankiang
Province; the Tiehli Training Camp at Tiehli, north of
Harbin, in Pinkiang Province; the Poli Training Camp at
Taoshan near Poli, southeast of Chiamussu, in Sankiang
Province; and the Sunwu Training Camp at Sunwu, south
of

=== Page 347 === (confidence 1.00)
of Heiho, in Heiho Province. Later, in the summer of
1938, it was discovered that the construotion works at
the five training camps did not progress properly
to flood and other unavoidable ciroumstandes and
special training camps were established, one at mmrbin
and the other at Changtu, a short distance south
Ssupingkai in Fengtien Province. Two camps were later
added, one regular training camp at Tashihtu and bne
speeial training camp at Imienpo.
When this program for training Manchuria-bound
youth volunteer settlers was made known, so much intereet
was stired up throughout Japan that over 9,o00 young
people from all over the country applied for the first
quota of 5,O00. From these 7,700 applioants were select-
ed, of whom 6,500 entered the Uchihara Training Camp in
March, 1938, being the beginning of the fisoal year.
These first members were sent off to training camps in
Manchuria in April and May, and another 7, 500 entered the
Camp in May and additional 1,Ooo in June. These were
again sent out to Manchuria between June and July, and
their places were taken up at the Camp by an approrimate-
ly same number of young people. These young people
entered the training camps in Manchuria for a three-year
training and experience as follows up to the end of
December, 1938 (See above, the Japanese Immigration Map):

while ten scattering camps will be opened up ohiefly
for the purpose of permanent settlement with accommoda-

old and new, wil1 be made for an upward of 54, 500 youths.

=== Page 349 === (confidence 0.99)
Attaohment No. 3
(From THE JAPAN ADVERTISER of August 29, 1939)
JAPANESE SETTLERS DOUBLE DURING YEAR
Survey Indicates That Total of
30,783 Pioneers Are Now
Working in "Manohukuo"
Brides More Numerous
About 1,782 Leave This Country
During Past Year - Number
of Students Increase
The rate at which Japanese have been emigrating
to "Manchukuo" increased sharply during the past year,
23,371 leaving this country to settle on the Continent
and swelling the Japanese population in the seven-year-
old country to 50,783, according to an investigation
conducted in April of this year, according to the Asahi.
The settlers belong either to the young men's and
boys' volunteer corps, the mass settlers, the collective
settlers and the volunteer railway guard corps. The
young men's and boys' volunteer corps consists of 45
divisions with a total membership of 32,034, which is
the largest of the four organizations of settlers.
The second largest group is the mass settlers,
which has a membership of 12,266 families with a total
population of 22,260, divided up into 87 divisions.
Third on the list are the collective settlers, who are
divided into 58 groups, embracing 1,872 families with
a population of 5,043. The volunteer railway guard is
the smallest group with 23 divisions and 1,456 members.
The most important of the groups, according to the
journal, is the young men's and boys' volunteer corps,
which is designed to form the nucleus of the future
Japanese colony in the new State. Up to last year,
the organization had maintained small training stations
at Nonni, Sunku, Tiehlei, Poli and Ningar. During this
fiscal year, there were constructed four large training
stations, each accommodating more than 6,000 persons,
three special stations, where special education is pro-
vided, and 21 small training stations.
A very sharp increase in the number of so-called
"Continental brides" is also said to have indicated by
the recent survey. These brides are said to have
accounted for about 1,782 of the Japanese who left this
year to pioneer the new country. The total number of
girls who have cone to join Japanese settlers in
"Manchukuo" is placed at 4,123.
The

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.99)
The mumber of children of the mass settlors who
have reached school age this year is given as 5.535
as compared with 1,654 children who attained sohgol
last year. The educational department of the Japanese
Embassy in Hsinking and the Manchuria Development
Company, which is playing a leading role in the promo-
tion of the colonization of Manchuria, have provided
67 new schools with a total of 226 classes during this
fisoal year, while 30 other school buildings are in the
course of construction.
According to a telephone message to the Asahi from
Kasama, Ibaraki Prefecture, a second batch of 1,460 set-
tlers is leaving soon to join the young men's and boys'
volunteer corps in Manchuria. They are said to have
finished training at the Uchihara training station and
will leave in three groups, one of which, consisting of
760 members, will depart on September 16. They will be
followed by another group of 350 boys and will sail from
Tsuruga and Niigata.
